The balance identity gives \[n h_n(\delta_n+h_n)^\kappa=h_n^{-2\beta}.\] Thus, using \(h_n\le1\) and \(\beta>0\), \[C_T\exp\{-c_T n h_n(\delta_n+h_n)^\kappa\} \le \frac{C_T}{c_T}h_n^\beta .\] Indeed, with \(e=n h_n(\delta_n+h_n)^\kappa=h_n^{-2\beta}\), the elementary bound \(\exp(-c_Te)\le(c_Te)^{-1}\) gives \((C_T/c_T)h_n^{2\beta}\), and \(h_n^{2\beta}\le h_n^\beta\).

For any \(P\in\mathcal M\), the projection \(\Pi_{[0,1]}\) is one-Lipschitz and the finite sum over strata gives the deterministic decomposition \[\mathbb E_{P^{\otimes n}} \left|\widehat\theta^{\mathrm{TG}}_{n,B_n}-\theta_{\delta_n}(P)\right| \le \mathbb E|\widehat\nu_n-\nu_{\delta_n}(P)| + \sum_{x\in\mathcal X} \left\{ \mathbb E|\widehat b_x-p_xq_x(\delta_n)| + |p_xq_x(\delta_n)| \mathbb E|\widetilde\mu_x-\mu_x^P(\delta_n)| \right\},\] where all expectations on the right are under \(P^{\otimes n}\). The retained-course and atom-mass block averages are averages of \([0,1]\)-valued variables, hence \[\mathbb E|\widehat\nu_n-\nu_{\delta_n}(P)| \le \frac12 |I_{0,n}|^{-1/2}, \qquad \mathbb E|\widehat b_x-p_xq_x(\delta_n)| \le \frac12 |I_{1,n}|^{-1/2}.\]

By Proposition 6 and \(0\le p_x\le1\), \[|p_xq_x(\delta_n)| \le \frac{c_+\delta_n^{\kappa+1}}{\kappa+1} = K_{\mathrm{atom}}\delta_n^{\kappa+1}.\]

It remains to bound the local regression term. On \(\Omega_x\), polynomial reproduction and the deterministic weight bound in Lemma 12 give the Hölder bias bound \[\left| \sum_{i\in I_{2,n}}w_{ix}\mu_X^P(A_i)-\mu_x^P(\delta_n) \right| \le LK_{\mathrm{bias}}h_n^\beta .\]

The centered noise term satisfies \[\mathbb E \left| \sum_{i\in I_{2,n}}w_{ix} \{Y_i-\mu_X^P(A_i)\} \right| \le K_{\mathrm{noise}}h_n^\beta,\] because the conditional variance is bounded by \(\frac14\sum_i w_{ix}^2\) and Lemma 13 controls the integrated square-root weight energy. On \(\Omega_x^c\), both the fallback value and \(\mu_x^P(\delta_n)\) lie in \([0,1]\), so the exceptional contribution is at most \(P^{\otimes n}(\Omega_x^c)\). Combining the good-event decomposition with the noise, bias, and tail bounds gives \[\mathbb E|\widetilde\mu_x-\mu_x^P(\delta_n)| \le K_{\mathrm{noise}}h_n^\beta+LK_{\mathrm{bias}}h_n^\beta +C_T\exp\{-c_T n h_n(\delta_n+h_n)^\kappa\} \le K_{\mathrm{loc}}h_n^\beta .\]

Before the split-size and tail simplifications, the finite-sample bound is \[\mathbb E_{P^{\otimes n}} \left|\widehat\theta^{\mathrm{TG}}_{n,B_n}-\theta_{\delta_n}(P)\right| \le \frac12 |I_{0,n}|^{-1/2} + J\left\{ \frac12 |I_{1,n}|^{-1/2} + K_{\mathrm{atom}}\delta_n^{\kappa+1} \left( K_{\mathrm{noise}}h_n^\beta+LK_{\mathrm{bias}}h_n^\beta +C_T\exp\{-c_T n h_n(\delta_n+h_n)^\kappa\} \right) \right\}.\]

Using the two split-block inequalities above and the local bound just obtained, uniformly in \(P\in\mathcal M\), \[\mathbb E_{P^{\otimes n}} \left|\widehat\theta^{\mathrm{TG}}_{n,B_n}-\theta_{\delta_n}(P)\right| \le 2r_{0,n} + J\{2r_{0,n}+K_{\mathrm{atom}}K_{\mathrm{loc}}a_n\}.\]

The model class is nonempty. Take the centered Bernoulli law with \(p_x=1/J\), conditional treatment density \(\pi_x(a)=(\kappa+1)a^\kappa\), and outcome distribution Bernoulli with mean \(1/2\) conditional on \((X,A)\); this law has regression \(\mu_x^P\equiv1/2\) and belongs to \(\mathcal M\) under the regime assumptions. Hence the same upper bound applies to the supremum over \(P\in\mathcal M\). Since \(r_{0,n},a_n\ge0\), \[2r_{0,n} + J\{2r_{0,n}+K_{\mathrm{atom}}K_{\mathrm{loc}}a_n\} \le 2(1+J)(1+K_{\mathrm{atom}}K_{\mathrm{loc}})(r_{0,n}+a_n).\]

Finally, \(r_{0,n}+a_n\) is exactly the frontier rate in Definition 9. This proves the asserted bound with the displayed \(C_{\mathrm{up}}\).

∎

Lemma 8 states the monomial reproduction property in the same notation used by the estimator. This property turns local-polynomial weighting into an estimate of the regression value at the threshold.

Lemma 16 [lem:bandwidth-phases] (Bandwidth phase bounds).

There exist constants \(c_{\mathrm{bal}},C_{\mathrm{bal}},c_{\mathrm{far}},C_{\mathrm{far}}\) with \[0<c_{\mathrm{bal}}\le C_{\mathrm{bal}}, \qquad 0<c_{\mathrm{far}}\le C_{\mathrm{far}},\] such that the following assertions hold. Let \[\delta_{\mathrm{edge},n} = n^{-1/(2\beta+\kappa+1)} .\] For every \(J\), every observed law \(P\), every \(L,c_-,c_+,p_{\min},\alpha\), and every deterministic sequence \((\delta_n)_{n\ge1}\), suppose that:

  • (Regime constants.) \(J\ge1\), \(\beta>0\), \(\kappa\ge0\), \(L>0\), \(c_->0\), \(c_-\le \kappa+1\le c_+\), \(p_{\min}>0\), \(p_{\min}\le 1/J\), \(0<\bar\delta<1\), and \(0<\alpha<1/2\).

  • (Thinning.) The law \(P\) satisfies the polynomial thinning condition in Assumption 4 with exponent \(\kappa\) and constants \(c_-,c_+\).

  • (Thresholds.) \(\delta_n\in[0,\bar\delta]\) for every \(n\).

Let \(h_n\) be the information-balance bandwidth from Definition 8 for \((n,\delta_n,\beta,\kappa,\bar\delta)\). Then, for all sufficiently large \(n\), \[c_{\mathrm{bal}} \le n h_n^{2\beta+1}(\delta_n+h_n)^\kappa \le C_{\mathrm{bal}},\] and \[c_{\mathrm{bal}}h_n^{-2\beta} \le n h_n(\delta_n+h_n)^\kappa \le C_{\mathrm{bal}}h_n^{-2\beta}.\] If \(\delta_n/\delta_{\mathrm{edge},n}\to\infty\), then, for all sufficiently large \(n\), \[c_{\mathrm{far}}\bigl(n\delta_n^\kappa\bigr)^{-1/(2\beta+1)} \le h_n \le C_{\mathrm{far}}\bigl(n\delta_n^\kappa\bigr)^{-1/(2\beta+1)} .\]

Moreover, for every \(D>0\), there exist constants \(c_{\mathrm{near}},C_{\mathrm{near}}\), depending on \(D\), with \[0<c_{\mathrm{near}}\le C_{\mathrm{near}},\] such that, under the same regime-constant, thinning, and threshold conditions, if \[|\delta_n|\le D|\delta_{\mathrm{edge},n}|\] for all sufficiently large \(n\), then, for all sufficiently large \(n\), \[c_{\mathrm{near}}n^{-1/(2\beta+\kappa+1)} \le h_n \le C_{\mathrm{near}}n^{-1/(2\beta+\kappa+1)} .\]

⊢ Lean
Proof of Lemma 16.

Set \[p=2\beta+1, \qquad c_{\mathrm{bal}}=C_{\mathrm{bal}}=1, \qquad C_{\mathrm{far}}=1,\] and choose \[c_{\mathrm{far}}=\min\{1,2^{-\kappa/p}\}.\] At this initial choice of witnesses, the quotient \(-\kappa/p\) is interpreted by the total real-division convention: it equals \(0\) when \(p=0\) and agrees with ordinary division when \(p\ne0\). Thus \(c_{\mathrm{far}}\) is a real constant for every value of \(\beta\). Since the base \(2\) is positive, \(2^{-\kappa/p}>0\), and therefore \[0<c_{\mathrm{far}}\le1=C_{\mathrm{far}}.\] Together with \(c_{\mathrm{bal}}=C_{\mathrm{bal}}=1\), this gives \[0<c_{\mathrm{bal}}\le C_{\mathrm{bal}}, \qquad 0<c_{\mathrm{far}}\le C_{\mathrm{far}}.\]

Fix \(J,P,L,c_-,c_+,p_{\min},\alpha\) and a deterministic threshold sequence satisfying the hypotheses. The regime assumptions give \(\beta>0\), \(\kappa\ge0\), and \(0<\bar\delta<1\), hence \(p=2\beta+1>0\). Let \(h_n\) be the information-balance bandwidth from Definition 8; thus \[h_n=\inf\{h\in(0,1-\bar\delta]: n h^{2\beta+1}(\delta_n+h)^\kappa\ge1\}\] when the displayed set is nonempty, with fallback value \(h_n=1-\bar\delta\) when that set is empty. By Lemma 10, for all sufficiently large \(n\), \[0<h_n, \qquad h_n\le1-\bar\delta, \qquad n h_n^{p}(\delta_n+h_n)^\kappa=1 .\]

The first balance assertion follows immediately: \[1 \le n h_n^{2\beta+1}(\delta_n+h_n)^\kappa \le 1 .\]

Since \(h_n>0\), \[h_n^{2\beta+1}=h_n^{2\beta}h_n, \qquad h_n^{-2\beta}=(h_n^{2\beta})^{-1}.\] Dividing the balance identity by \(h_n^{2\beta}\) gives \[n h_n(\delta_n+h_n)^\kappa=h_n^{-2\beta},\] and therefore \[h_n^{-2\beta} \le n h_n(\delta_n+h_n)^\kappa \le h_n^{-2\beta}.\]

Now assume \[\frac{\delta_n}{\delta_{\mathrm{edge},n}}\to\infty, \qquad \delta_{\mathrm{edge},n}=n^{-1/(2\beta+\kappa+1)}=n^{-1/(p+\kappa)}.\] On a sufficiently large eventual set, the balance identity holds, \(n>0\), and the convergence gives \(1\le\delta_n/\delta_{\mathrm{edge},n}\). Since \(\delta_{\mathrm{edge},n}>0\) on this set, \[\delta_{\mathrm{edge},n}\le\delta_n, \qquad \delta_n>0.\]

For those \(n\), \(\delta_n^\kappa\le(\delta_n+h_n)^\kappa\), so the balance identity implies \[h_n^p(n\delta_n^\kappa)\le1.\] Taking \(p\)-th roots gives \[h_n\le (n\delta_n^\kappa)^{-1/p} .\]

The comparison \(\delta_{\mathrm{edge},n}\le\delta_n\) gives \[1=n\delta_{\mathrm{edge},n}^{p+\kappa} \le n\delta_n^{p+\kappa} =\delta_n^p(n\delta_n^\kappa),\] and hence \[(n\delta_n^\kappa)^{-1/p}\le\delta_n .\]

Consequently \(h_n\le\delta_n\), and therefore \[\delta_n+h_n\le2\delta_n .\] Using the balance identity again, \[1 = n h_n^p(\delta_n+h_n)^\kappa \le n h_n^p(2\delta_n)^\kappa = h_n^p\,2^\kappa(n\delta_n^\kappa).\] Thus \[2^{-\kappa/p}(n\delta_n^\kappa)^{-1/p}\le h_n .\]

Under \(p>0\) and \(\kappa\ge0\), \(2^{-\kappa/p}\le1\), so the chosen constant satisfies \(c_{\mathrm{far}}=2^{-\kappa/p}\). With \(C_{\mathrm{far}}=1\), the far-threshold comparison follows: \[c_{\mathrm{far}}(n\delta_n^\kappa)^{-1/(2\beta+1)} \le h_n \le C_{\mathrm{far}}(n\delta_n^\kappa)^{-1/(2\beta+1)} .\]

Finally fix \(D>0\). Set \[C_{\mathrm{near}}=1, \qquad c_{\mathrm{near}}=\min\{1,(D+1)^{-\kappa/p}\},\] again using the same total real-division convention for the displayed quotient. Since \(D+1>0\), the positive-base property of real powers gives \((D+1)^{-\kappa/p}>0\), and therefore \[0<c_{\mathrm{near}}\le1=C_{\mathrm{near}}.\] Fix again \(J,P,L,c_-,c_+,p_{\min},\alpha\) and a deterministic threshold sequence satisfying the hypotheses, and let \(h_n\) be the corresponding bandwidth from Definition 8. The regime assumptions again give \(p>0\) and \(\kappa\ge0\). Suppose that eventually \[|\delta_n|\le D|\delta_{\mathrm{edge},n}|.\] On the eventual set where this bound and Lemma 10 both hold, \(n>0\), \(\delta_n\ge0\), and \(\delta_{\mathrm{edge},n}>0\), so \[\delta_n\le D n^{-1/(p+\kappa)}.\]

The balance identity and \(h_n^\kappa\le(\delta_n+h_n)^\kappa\) give \[n h_n^{p+\kappa}\le1,\] hence \[h_n\le n^{-1/(p+\kappa)}.\]

Together with the threshold bound, this gives \[\delta_n+h_n\le(D+1)n^{-1/(p+\kappa)}.\] Therefore \[1 = n h_n^p(\delta_n+h_n)^\kappa \le n h_n^p (D+1)^\kappa\bigl(n^{-1/(p+\kappa)}\bigr)^\kappa .\] Since \(n\bigl(n^{-1/(p+\kappa)}\bigr)^{p+\kappa}=1\), the displayed inequality \(1\le n h_n^p (D+1)^\kappa (n^{-1/(p+\kappa)})^\kappa\) rearranges to \[(D+1)^{-\kappa/p}n^{-1/(p+\kappa)}\le h_n .\]

Under \(p>0\), \(\kappa\ge0\), and \(D>0\), \((D+1)^{-\kappa/p}\le1\), so the chosen constant satisfies \(c_{\mathrm{near}}=(D+1)^{-\kappa/p}\). Since \(p+\kappa=2\beta+\kappa+1\), we obtain, for all sufficiently large \(n\), \[c_{\mathrm{near}}n^{-1/(2\beta+\kappa+1)} \le h_n \le C_{\mathrm{near}}n^{-1/(2\beta+\kappa+1)} .\]

∎

Lemma 9 gives the pointwise weight control implied by Gram coercivity. Together with Lemma 12, it bounds both individual leverage and aggregate variance.

Lemma 17 [lem:total-gram] (Total Gram stabilization).

Let \(J\) be a positive integer and let the declared constants satisfy \[\beta>0,\qquad \kappa\ge 0,\qquad L>0,\qquad 0<c_-\le \kappa+1\le c_+,\qquad 0<p_{\min}\le 1/J,\qquad 0<\bar\delta<1,\qquad 0<\alpha<1/2 .\] Then there are constants \(C,c>0\), depending only on these declared constants, such that the uniform population Gram lower-bound constant \(\lambda_\star\) in Definition 12, evaluated at \((\ell,\kappa,c_-,c_+)\) with \(\ell=\lceil\beta\rceil-1\), satisfies \(\lambda_\star>0\), and the following hold.

  • (Model and sampling.) \(P\in\mathcal M\) as in Definition 5, and \(O_1,\ldots,O_n\) are sampled according to Assumption 1.

  • (Split and threshold.) \(I_0,I_1,I_2\subset\{1,\ldots,n\}\) are deterministic, pairwise-disjoint split blocks with \(|I_b|\ge \lfloor n/4\rfloor\) for \(b=0,1,2\), and \(\delta\in[0,\bar\delta]\).

  • (Bandwidth and stratum.) \(h_n\) is the information-balance bandwidth from Definition 8 at \((n,\delta,\beta,\kappa,\bar\delta)\), and \(x\in\mathcal X\).

For the good-design event \(\Omega_x\) in Definition 12, \[P^{\otimes n}(\Omega_x^c) \le C\exp\{-c\,n\,h_n(\delta+h_n)^\kappa\}.\] On \(\Omega_x\), the exact local-polynomial intercept weights \(w_{ix}\) in Definition 12 satisfy, for every \(j=0,\ldots,\ell\), \[\sum_{i\in I_2} w_{ix} U_i^j = \begin{cases} 1, & j=0,\\ 0, & j\ge 1, \end{cases}\] and also obey \[\sum_{i\in I_2}|w_{ix}|\le C, \qquad \sum_{i\in I_2}w_{ix}^2\le \frac{C}{N_x}.\]

⊢ Lean
Proof of Lemma 17.

Set \[\ell=\lceil\beta\rceil-1,\qquad \lambda=\lambda_\star(\ell,\kappa,c_-,c_+),\qquad d=\ell+1 .\] The regime assumptions give \(\kappa\ge0\), \(c_->0\), and \(c_+>0\), so \(\lambda>0\) by Lemma 2.

Define \[\eta= \min\left\{ \frac1{20}, \frac{\lambda^2}{16d(4d+\lambda)} \right\}, \qquad K=\frac{p_{\min}c_-}{2\cdot2^\kappa}, \qquad c=\frac{\eta K}{8},\] and \[A_{\mathrm{tail}}=2(1+d^2),\qquad C= A_{\mathrm{tail}}e^{8c}+\frac{2\sqrt d}{\lambda}+\frac{4d}{\lambda^2}+1 .\] The constants \(\eta,K,c,A_{\mathrm{tail}},C\) are positive and, together with \(\lambda\), depend only on the displayed regime constants.

Fix \(P,n,B_n,\delta,x\) as in the statement, and write \[h=h_n, \qquad q(O)=\mathbf 1\left\{X=x,\ 0\le\frac{A-\delta}{h}\le1\right\}, \qquad \phi_j(O)= \mathbf 1\left\{0\le\frac{A-\delta}{h}\le1\right\} \left(\frac{A-\delta}{h}\right)^j .\] The regime assumptions give \(\beta>0\), \(\kappa\ge0\), \(\delta\in[0,\bar\delta]\), and \(0<\bar\delta<1\). The construction of \(h\) in Definition 8 gives \(0<h\le1-\bar\delta\). Indeed, if the crossing set in Definition 8 is empty, then \(h=1-\bar\delta\). If it is nonempty and \(z\) is a member, then \(n>0\), \(0<z\le1-\bar\delta\), \(\delta+z\le1\), and \[1\le n z^{2\beta+1}(\delta+z)^\kappa\le n z^{2\beta+1},\] so \(n^{-1/(2\beta+1)}\le z\). Hence the infimum is bounded below by this positive number and above by any member of the set, giving \(0<h\le1-\bar\delta\). Since \(\delta\le\bar\delta\), this also gives \(\delta+h\le1\).

Let \[p=\mathbb E_P q(O) = P\left\{X=x,\ 0\le\frac{A-\delta}{h}\le1\right\}.\] By Lemmas 4 and 3 and the thinning and stratum-mass assumptions, \[p = p_x\int_{\delta}^{\delta+h}\pi_x(a)\,da \ge p_{\min}c_-\int_{\delta}^{\delta+h}a^\kappa\,da \ge p_{\min}c_-\frac h2\left(\frac{\delta+h}{2}\right)^\kappa = K\,h(\delta+h)^\kappa .\]

For every \(b\in\mathbb R^{\ell+1}\), Lemma 11 gives \[\lambda\,p\,\|b\|_2^2 \le \sum_{j=0}^{\ell}\sum_{k=0}^{\ell} b_jb_k\,\mathbb E_P\{q(O)\phi_j(O)\phi_k(O)\}.\] Moreover \(0\le q\le1\) and \(|\phi_j|\le1\) for every \(j\).

Let \(m=|I_2|\), and let \(i_1,\ldots,i_m\) be the elements of \(I_2\) in increasing order. For a block vector \(\omega=(\omega_1,\ldots,\omega_m)\), define \[N_{\mathrm{blk}}(\omega)=\sum_{r=1}^m q(\omega_r),\qquad M_{\mathrm{blk},jk}(\omega)=\sum_{r=1}^m q(\omega_r)\phi_j(\omega_r)\phi_k(\omega_r),\] and put \[M_{jk}=\mathbb E_P\{q(O)\phi_j(O)\phi_k(O)\}.\] The deterministic ordered-block map \[T_{I_2}(O_1,\ldots,O_n)=(O_{i_1},\ldots,O_{i_m})\] satisfies, for every measurable block event \(E\), \[P^{\otimes n}\{T_{I_2}(O_1,\ldots,O_n)\in E\}=P^{\otimes m}(E),\] because the selected coordinates are independent and each has law \(P\). Under this map, \[N_{\mathrm{blk}}(T_{I_2}z) = \sum_{i\in I_2}q(O_i)=N_x,\] and, for every \(j,k\), \[M_{\mathrm{blk},jk}(T_{I_2}z) = \sum_{i\in I_2}q(O_i)\phi_j(O_i)\phi_k(O_i) =(G_x)_{jk},\] where the last equality is the entrywise definition of the local Gram matrix in Definition 12.

Define the block good event \[\Gamma_x= \left\{ \omega: N_{\mathrm{blk}}(\omega)>0 \ \text{and}\ \sum_{j=0}^{\ell}\sum_{k=0}^{\ell} b_jb_k M_{\mathrm{blk},jk}(\omega) \ge \frac{\lambda N_{\mathrm{blk}}(\omega)}{2}\|b\|_2^2 \ \text{for every } b\in\mathbb R^d \right\}.\] The preceding identities give the exact event identity \[\Omega_x=\{z:T_{I_2}z\in\Gamma_x\}.\] Indeed, \(q\) is the indicator of the stratum-window event, \(\phi_j\) is its local monomial coordinate, and \(\lambda=\lambda_\star(\ell,\kappa,c_-,c_+)\), so \(N_{\mathrm{blk}}\) and \(M_{\mathrm{blk}}\) are exactly the count and quadratic form appearing in Definition 12.

If \(n\ge8\), the split condition gives \(m\ge n/8\), hence \(m>0\). The lower bound on \(p\) gives \(p>0\). Applying the localized empirical Gram concentration bound to the \(m\) independent block coordinates, with the population coercivity above and with \(0\le q\le1\) and \(|\phi_j|\le1\), gives \[P^{\otimes m}(\Gamma_x^c) \le A_{\mathrm{tail}}\exp\{-mp\eta\}.\]

By the ordered-block product identity and \(\Omega_x=\{z:T_{I_2}z\in\Gamma_x\}\), \[P^{\otimes n}(\Omega_x^c) \le A_{\mathrm{tail}}\exp\{-mp\eta\}.\] Combining \(m\ge n/8\) with the lower bound on \(p\), \[c\,n\,h(\delta+h)^\kappa \le m\,p\,\eta .\] Hence \[P^{\otimes n}(\Omega_x^c) \le A_{\mathrm{tail}}\exp\{-mp\eta\} \le A_{\mathrm{tail}}\exp\{-c\,n\,h(\delta+h)^\kappa\} \le C\exp\{-c\,n\,h(\delta+h)^\kappa\}.\]

If \(n<8\), then \(h\le1\), \(\delta+h\le1\), and \(\kappa\ge0\) imply \[n\,h(\delta+h)^\kappa\le8.\] Thus \[1\le A_{\mathrm{tail}}e^{8c}\exp\{-c\,n\,h(\delta+h)^\kappa\} \le C\exp\{-c\,n\,h(\delta+h)^\kappa\},\] and the same probability bound follows from \(P^{\otimes n}(\Omega_x^c)\le1\).

On \(\Omega_x\), Lemma 12 gives, for every \(0\le j\le\ell\), \[\sum_{i\in I_2}w_{ix}U_i^j = \mathbf 1\{j=0\},\] which is the displayed reproduction identity. The same result also gives \[\sum_{i\in I_2}|w_{ix}| \le \frac{2\sqrt d}{\lambda} \le C, \qquad \sum_{i\in I_2}w_{ix}^2 \le \frac{4d}{\lambda^2N_x} \le \frac{C}{N_x}.\]

∎

Lemma 17 assembles the local-window identities, shifted coercivity, and concentration into the good-design probability bound used by the stabilized procedures. The exponential bound makes the singular-design fallback negligible on the scales used in Theorems 1 and 2, while the on-event identities preserve the usual local-polynomial intercept behavior.

The last auxiliary lemma for the appendix supports the continuity-only causal and observed targets. It establishes pointwise uniqueness of the continuous conditional-regression extension on the threshold envelope.

Lemma 18 [lem:continuity-regression-extension-unique] (Unique continuous regression extension).

Let \(J\) be a positive integer, let \(\kappa,c_-,c_+,p_{\min},\bar\delta\in\mathbb R\), and let \(P\in\mathcal M_{\mathrm{cont}}\) be a continuity-only observed clamp model with these constants, as in Definition 24. Suppose that

  • (Design constants.) The constants satisfy \[0\leq \kappa,\qquad 0<c_-,\qquad c_-\leq \kappa+1\leq c_+,\qquad 0<p_{\min}\leq \frac1J,\qquad 0<\bar\delta<1 .\]

  • (Continuous versions.) For \(r=1,2\), \(\mu_r:\mathcal X\times\mathbb R\to\mathbb R\) satisfies that \(a\mapsto \mu_r(x,a)\) is continuous on \([0,\bar\delta]\) for every \(x\in\mathcal X\).

  • (Regression version property.) For \(r=1,2\), \[\mathbb E_P[Y\mid X,A]=\mu_r(X,A) \quad\text{\(P\)-almost surely.}\]

Then, for every \(x\in\mathcal X\), \[\mu_1(x,a)=\mu_2(x,a) \qquad\text{for every }a\in[0,\bar\delta].\]

⊢ Lean
Proof of Lemma 18.

Fix \(x\in\mathcal X\). The two regression-version assumptions imply \[\mu_1(X,A)=\mu_2(X,A) \quad P\text{-a.s.}\] Define functions on the real line by \[f_r(a)= \begin{cases} \mu_r(x,a),&a\in[0,\bar\delta],\\ 0,&a\notin[0,\bar\delta], \end{cases} \qquad r=1,2.\] The continuity hypotheses make these functions measurable. Set \[B=\{a\in\mathbb R:f_1(a)\neq f_2(a)\}.\] Then \(B\) is measurable and \[B\subset[0,\bar\delta]\subset[0,1].\] The almost-sure equality of the two versions gives \[P\{X=x,\ A\in B\}=0.\] The conditional-density identity in Assumption 2 applies to this measurable subset of \([0,1]\), so \[0=P\{X=x,\ A\in B\} = p_x\int_B\pi_x(a)\,da.\] The stratum-mass condition in Assumption 3, together with \(p_{\min}>0\), gives \(p_x>0\). Hence \[\int_B\pi_x(a)\,da=0.\]

It remains to justify the zero-integral argument on \(B\). The conditional-density measurability in Assumption 2 gives the needed measurability on the restricted set. Since \(B\subset[0,1]\), the set \(B\) has finite Lebesgue measure. For Lebesgue-a.e. \(a\in B\), the nonnegativity in Assumption 2, the upper envelope in Assumption 4, and the design inequalities \(0\leq\kappa\) and \(0\leq c_+\) give \[|\pi_x(a)|=\pi_x(a) \leq c_+a^\kappa \leq c_+, \qquad 0\leq a\leq1 .\] Thus \(a\mapsto\pi_x(a)\) is integrable on \(B\). The nonnegativity part of Assumption 2 and the zero integral therefore yield \[\pi_x(a)=0 \quad\text{for Lebesgue-a.e. }a\in B.\] The point \(a=0\) has Lebesgue measure zero. For Lebesgue-a.e. \(a\in B\) with \(a\neq0\), the inclusion \(B\subset[0,1]\) gives \(a>0\). The lower envelope in Assumption 4 and \(c_->0\) then give \[\pi_x(a)\geq c_-a^\kappa>0 \quad\text{for Lebesgue-a.e. }a\in B\setminus\{0\}.\] Combining this positivity with \(\pi_x=0\) Lebesgue-a.e. on \(B\) gives \[\operatorname{Leb}(B)=0.\] Consequently \(f_1=f_2\) Lebesgue-a.e. on \(\mathbb R\), and the definitions of \(f_1\) and \(f_2\) give \[\mu_1(x,a)=\mu_2(x,a) \quad\text{for Lebesgue-a.e. }a\in[0,\bar\delta].\]

Finally, both functions \(a\mapsto\mu_1(x,a)\) and \(a\mapsto\mu_2(x,a)\) are continuous on \([0,\bar\delta]\). If they differed at some point of \([0,\bar\delta]\), continuity would make them differ on a nonempty relatively open interval in \([0,\bar\delta]\), which has positive Lebesgue measure, contradicting the almost-everywhere equality on \([0,\bar\delta]\). Hence \[\mu_1(x,a)=\mu_2(x,a) \qquad\text{for every }a\in[0,\bar\delta].\] Since \(x\) was arbitrary, the conclusion holds in every stratum.

∎

Lemma 18 justifies the pointwise notation for the continuity-only regression extension used in Definition 25. The positive treatment-density envelope gives enough support throughout \([0,\bar\delta]\) for almost-everywhere equality of continuous versions to determine the extension at every threshold value.

Dependencies. A successful build is not by itself the claim made here: the reported status additionally requires that the sources contain no sorry, no admit, and no added axiom, and that #print axioms on each anchored declaration reports only the three standard Lean axioms. Both checks are scripted in the repository and are rerun against the archived commit.

Anchor-to-declaration manifest. The bundle accompanying this manuscript ships a machine-readable crosswalk that maps every numbered environment in the paper to the declaration that certifies it. A companion file records, for each anchor, the verbatim source text of the certifying declaration, so a reader can compare a displayed statement with the checked one without rebuilding the library. The interactive edition of this paper renders that manifest inline at each numbered environment.

Proofs of the main results

Proof of Theorem 1.

Fix the displayed regime constants. We use seven steps.

  1. Write \(R_n^\star\) for the observed minimax risk over the admissible estimator class used here, \[R_n^\star = \inf_{T_n} \sup_{P\in\mathcal M} \mathbb E_{P^{\otimes n}} \left[ \left|T_n-\theta_{\delta_n}(P)\right| \right],\] where the infimum ranges over observed-sample measurable estimators satisfying \(T_n(z)\in[0,1]\) for every observed sample \(z\). For a split \(B_n\), put \[\mathcal R_n^{\mathrm{TG}}(B_n) = \sup_{P\in\mathcal M} \mathbb E_{P^{\otimes n}} \left[ \left|\widehat\theta_{n,B_n}^{\mathrm{TG}}-\theta_{\delta_n}(P)\right| \right].\] This is the finite-sample stabilized risk in Definition 16, with the product sample law interpreted as in Definition 2. Lemma 15 gives a constant \(C_{\mathrm{up}}>0\) such that, for every admissible deterministic \((\delta_n)\) and \(B_\bullet\), for all sufficiently large \(n\), \[\mathcal R_n^{\mathrm{TG}}(B_n) \le C_{\mathrm{up}} \left(n^{-1/2}+\delta_n^{\kappa+1}h_n^\beta\right).\] The estimator \(\widehat\theta_{n,B_n}^{\mathrm{TG}}\) is observed-sample measurable and \([0,1]\)-valued. Hence it is an admissible competitor in the infimum defining \(R_n^\star\), so \[R_n^\star\le \mathcal R_n^{\mathrm{TG}}(B_n).\]

  2. We record the two-point device used for both lower bounds. Let \(H_0,H_1\in\mathcal M\), put \(\vartheta_j=\theta_\delta(H_j)\), and suppose \[0\le \Delta\le |\vartheta_1-\vartheta_0|, \qquad 0\le\delta\le1,\] with well-posed product chi-squared divergence \[H_1^{\otimes n}\ll H_0^{\otimes n}, \qquad \int\left(\frac{dH_1^{\otimes n}}{dH_0^{\otimes n}}-1\right)^2dH_0^{\otimes n}<\infty,\] and \[\chi^2(H_1^{\otimes n},H_0^{\otimes n})\le \chi, \qquad 0\le\chi<4 .\] For any admissible \([0,1]\)-valued observed-sample estimator \(T_n\), the testing inequality and \(\|H_1^{\otimes n}-H_0^{\otimes n}\|_{\mathrm{TV}} \le \frac12\sqrt{\chi}\) give \[\max_{j=0,1} H_j^{\otimes n} \left\{ |T_n-\vartheta_j|\ge \Delta/2 \right\} \ge \frac{1-\frac12\sqrt{\chi}}{2}.\] Multiplying by \(\Delta/2\), the larger of the two risks under \(H_0,H_1\) is at least \[\frac{1-\frac12\sqrt{\chi}}{4}\,\Delta .\] The supremum over \(P\in\mathcal M\) in the restricted minimax problem dominates these two risks for each \(T_n\); taking the infimum over admissible \(T_n\) yields \[R_n^\star \ge \frac{1-\frac12\sqrt{\chi}}{4}\,\Delta .\]

  3. For the parametric component, use the canonical design \[P(X=x)=1/J, \qquad A\mid X=x\sim (\kappa+1)a^\kappa\,da, \quad a\in[0,1],\] and Bernoulli regressions \[\mu_{x}^{P_{\mathrm r,0}}(a)=\frac12, \qquad \mu_{x}^{P_{\mathrm r,1}}(a)=\frac12+\varepsilon_n, \qquad \varepsilon_n=\frac14 n^{-1/2}.\] Both laws belong to \(\mathcal M\). The stratum masses are \(1/J\), which meets the stratum-mass condition of Assumption 3 under the regime constraint \(p_{\min}\le1/J\); the canonical density \((\kappa+1)a^\kappa\) meets the conditional-density condition of Assumption 2 and, by the regime constraint \(c_-\le\kappa+1\le c_+\), the polynomial-thinning envelope of Assumption 4; and each regression is constant, so every Taylor remainder in Assumption 5 vanishes and the Hölder condition holds for any \(L>0\). Finally \(|\varepsilon_n|\le1/4\) for all \(n\ge1\), so both constant regressions take values in \([0,1]\). The clamp target shifts by \[\left| \theta_{\delta_n}(P_{\mathrm r,1}) - \theta_{\delta_n}(P_{\mathrm r,0}) \right| =\varepsilon_n .\] The product chi-squared divergence is well posed, and \[1+\chi^2(P_{\mathrm r,1}^{\otimes n},P_{\mathrm r,0}^{\otimes n}) = (1+4\varepsilon_n^2)^n \le \exp(n\,4\varepsilon_n^2) = \exp(1/4).\] With \(\chi_{\mathrm r}=\exp(1/4)-1<4\), the two-point mixture bound \((1+4\varepsilon_n^2)^n\le\exp(1/4)\) gives a constant \(c_{\mathrm r}>0\), depending only on the regime constants, such that \[c_{\mathrm r}n^{-1/2}\le R_n^\star\] for every admissible \((\delta_n)\) and all sufficiently large \(n\).

  4. For the localized lower-bound component, use a fixed \(C^\infty\) dose bump \(b_\circ:\mathbb R\to[0,1]\) centered at zero, with \[b_\circ(0)=1, \qquad 0\le b_\circ(u)\le1, \qquad b_\circ(u)=0\quad\text{for }u\notin[-1,1].\] Let \(\ell=\ell(\beta)\). Since \(\beta>0\), the number \(\gamma_\beta=\beta-\ell\) belongs to \((0,1]\). Because \(b_\circ\) is smooth and compactly supported, there is a finite constant \(K_{\mathrm{bump}}\ge1\) such that \[\left| b_\circ^{(\ell)}(u)-b_\circ^{(\ell)}(v) \right| \le K_{\mathrm{bump}}|u-v|^{\gamma_\beta}, \qquad u,v\in\mathbb R.\] Choose \(a_{\mathrm{low}}>0\) so small that \[a_{\mathrm{low}}\le\frac14, \qquad a_{\mathrm{low}}K_{\mathrm{bump}}\le L .\] For any \(0\le\delta\le1\) and \(0<h\le1\), define \[q_{\delta,h}^{\mathrm{low}}(a) = a_{\mathrm{low}}h^\beta b_\circ\!\left(\frac{a-\delta}{h}\right), \qquad f_{\delta,h}^{\mathrm{low}}(a) = \frac12+q_{\delta,h}^{\mathrm{low}}(a).\] Then \(f_{\delta,h}^{\mathrm{low}}\) is continuous on \([0,1]\), and \(f_{\delta,h}^{\mathrm{low}}(a)\in[0,1]\) there because \[0\le q_{\delta,h}^{\mathrm{low}}(a)\le a_{\mathrm{low}}h^\beta\le a_{\mathrm{low}}\le\frac14 .\] For \(s,t\in[0,1]\), differentiating the scaled bump \(\ell\) times and using \(\gamma_\beta=\beta-\ell\) gives \[\begin{aligned} \left| \frac{d^\ell}{da^\ell}q_{\delta,h}^{\mathrm{low}}(s) - \frac{d^\ell}{da^\ell}q_{\delta,h}^{\mathrm{low}}(t) \right| &\le a_{\mathrm{low}}h^{\beta-\ell}K_{\mathrm{bump}} \left|\frac{s-\delta}{h}-\frac{t-\delta}{h}\right|^{\gamma_\beta} \\ &= a_{\mathrm{low}}K_{\mathrm{bump}}|s-t|^{\beta-\ell} \le L|s-t|^{\beta-\ell}. \end{aligned}\] The one-dimensional Taylor remainder theorem on \([0,1]\) therefore yields \[\left| f_{\delta,h}^{\mathrm{low}}(t) - \sum_{j=0}^{\ell} \frac{(f_{\delta,h}^{\mathrm{low}})^{(j)}(s)}{j!}(t-s)^j \right| \le L|t-s|^\beta, \qquad s,t\in[0,1].\] Thus the localized Bernoulli regression satisfies the member condition in Assumption 5 uniformly over \(0\le\delta\le1\) and \(0<h\le1\). Set \[\zeta_{\mathrm{low}}=8(\kappa+1)a_{\mathrm{low}}^2, \qquad \rho_\beta=2\beta+1, \qquad \tau_{\mathrm{low}} = \exp\!\left(-\frac{\zeta_{\mathrm{low}}+1}{\rho_\beta}\right).\] Then \(0<\tau_{\mathrm{low}}\le1\) and \[\zeta_{\mathrm{low}}\tau_{\mathrm{low}}^{2\beta+1}\le1.\] For the contracted bandwidth \(\tau_{\mathrm{low}}h_n\), form Bernoulli laws \[\mu_x^{Q_{\mathrm{low},0}}(a)=\frac12, \qquad \mu_x^{Q_{\mathrm{low},1}}(a)=\frac12+ a_{\mathrm{low}}(\tau_{\mathrm{low}}h_n)^\beta b_\circ\!\left(\frac{a-\delta_n}{\tau_{\mathrm{low}}h_n}\right)\] on the same canonical design. For all sufficiently large \(n\), Lemma 10 gives \[0<h_n, \qquad h_n\le1-\bar\delta, \qquad n h_n^{2\beta+1}(\delta_n+h_n)^\kappa=1 .\] Together with \(\delta_n\in[0,\bar\delta]\), this gives \(\delta_n\in[0,1]\), \(0<\tau_{\mathrm{low}}h_n\le h_n\le1\), and \(\delta_n+h_n\le1\). The displayed Taylor bound places both laws in \(\mathcal M\). The target separation obeys \[a_{\mathrm{low}}\delta_n^{\kappa+1}(\tau_{\mathrm{low}}h_n)^\beta \le \left| \theta_{\delta_n}(Q_{\mathrm{low},1}) - \theta_{\delta_n}(Q_{\mathrm{low},0}) \right|.\] The one-observation chi-squared increment satisfies \[\int 4a_{\mathrm{low}}^2(\tau_{\mathrm{low}}h_n)^{2\beta} b_\circ\!\left(\frac{a-\delta_n}{\tau_{\mathrm{low}}h_n}\right)^2 \,dP_{X,A}(x,a) \le 8(\kappa+1)a_{\mathrm{low}}^2 (\tau_{\mathrm{low}}h_n)^{2\beta+1}(\delta_n+\tau_{\mathrm{low}}h_n)^\kappa .\] Using the balance identity above, \(\tau_{\mathrm{low}}h_n\le h_n\), and \(\kappa\ge0\), \[n\int 4a_{\mathrm{low}}^2(\tau_{\mathrm{low}}h_n)^{2\beta} b_\circ\!\left(\frac{a-\delta_n}{\tau_{\mathrm{low}}h_n}\right)^2 \,dP_{X,A}(x,a) \le \zeta_{\mathrm{low}}\tau_{\mathrm{low}}^{2\beta+1} \le1 .\] Let \[I_{\mathrm{low},n} = \int 4a_{\mathrm{low}}^2(\tau_{\mathrm{low}}h_n)^{2\beta} b_\circ\!\left(\frac{a-\delta_n}{\tau_{\mathrm{low}}h_n}\right)^2 \,dP_{X,A}(x,a)\] be the one-observation chi-squared increment for this Bernoulli perturbation. The product law factorization gives \[1+\chi^2(Q_{\mathrm{low},1}^{\otimes n},Q_{\mathrm{low},0}^{\otimes n}) = (1+I_{\mathrm{low},n})^n .\] Since \(I_{\mathrm{low},n}\ge0\) and \(nI_{\mathrm{low},n}\le1\), the inequality \(1+u\le e^u\) yields \[\chi^2(Q_{\mathrm{low},1}^{\otimes n},Q_{\mathrm{low},0}^{\otimes n}) \le \exp(nI_{\mathrm{low},n})-1 \le e-1<4 .\] Applying the two-point bound gives a constant \(c_{\mathrm{loc}}>0\) such that \[c_{\mathrm{loc}}\delta_n^{\kappa+1}h_n^\beta \le R_n^\star\] for every admissible \((\delta_n)\) and all sufficiently large \(n\).

  5. The global witness in the theorem uses the same canonical design and \[\mu_x^{P_0}(a)=\frac12, \qquad \mu_x^{P_1}(a)=\frac12+n^{-1/2}.\] For all sufficiently large \(n\), \(n^{-1/2}\le1/2\), so these are Bernoulli laws in \(\mathcal M\), with shared \((X,A)\)-design, shared \(p_x\), and shared \(\pi_x\). They satisfy \[\mu_x^{P_1}(a)=\mu_x^{P_0}(a)+n^{-1/2}\] for every \(x\) and \(a\in[0,1]\), and \[\frac12 n^{-1/2} \le \left| \theta_{\delta_n}(P_1)-\theta_{\delta_n}(P_0) \right|.\] The one-observation chi-squared increment is \(4n^{-1}\). Hence the product divergence is well posed and satisfies \[1+\chi^2(P_1^{\otimes n},P_0^{\otimes n}) = (1+4n^{-1})^n \le \exp(4),\] so \[\chi^2(P_1^{\otimes n},P_0^{\otimes n}) \le \exp(4).\]

  6. For the localized witness appearing in the statement, use the same fixed bump \(b_\circ\). The amplitude construction above also permits a separate witness amplitude: choose \(\eta_{\mathrm{wit}}>0\) such that \[\eta_{\mathrm{wit}}\le\frac14, \qquad \eta_{\mathrm{wit}}K_{\mathrm{bump}}\le L .\] For every \(0\le\delta\le1\) and \(0<h\le1\), the function \[f_{\delta,h}^{\mathrm{wit}}(t) = \frac12+\eta_{\mathrm{wit}} h^\beta b_\circ\!\left(\frac{t-\delta}{h}\right)\] is continuous on \([0,1]\), takes values in \([0,1]\), and the same scaled-derivative calculation gives \[\left| f_{\delta,h}^{\mathrm{wit}}(t) - \sum_{j=0}^{\ell} \frac{(f_{\delta,h}^{\mathrm{wit}})^{(j)}(s)}{j!}(t-s)^j \right| \le L|t-s|^\beta, \qquad s,t\in[0,1].\] Set \[\zeta_{\mathrm{wit}}=8(\kappa+1)\eta_{\mathrm{wit}}^2, \qquad D_{\mathrm{wit}}=\exp(\zeta_{\mathrm{wit}}).\] For \(h_n\) itself, define \[\mu_x^{Q_0}(t)=\frac12, \qquad \mu_x^{Q_1}(t)=\frac12+ \eta_{\mathrm{wit}} h_n^\beta b_\circ\!\left(\frac{t-\delta_n}{h_n}\right).\] For all sufficiently large \(n\), Lemma 10 gives \[0<h_n, \qquad h_n\le1-\bar\delta, \qquad n h_n^{2\beta+1}(\delta_n+h_n)^\kappa=1 .\] Together with \(\delta_n\in[0,\bar\delta]\), this gives \(\delta_n\in[0,1]\), \(h_n\le1\), and \(\delta_n+h_n\le1\), so \(Q_0,Q_1\in\mathcal M\). The pair has Bernoulli outcomes, common design, common \(p_x\), common \(\pi_x\), and \[\mu_x^{Q_1}(t)-\mu_x^{Q_0}(t) = \eta_{\mathrm{wit}} h_n^\beta b_\circ\!\left(\frac{t-\delta_n}{h_n}\right)\] for every \(x\) and \(t\in[0,1]\). Since \(b_\circ(0)=1\) and the perturbation is nonnegative, \[\eta_{\mathrm{wit}}\delta_n^{\kappa+1}h_n^\beta \le \left| \theta_{\delta_n}(Q_1)-\theta_{\delta_n}(Q_0) \right|.\] The same integral calculation and the balance identity give \[n\int 4\eta_{\mathrm{wit}}^2h_n^{2\beta} b_\circ\!\left(\frac{t-\delta_n}{h_n}\right)^2 \,dP_{X,A}(x,t) \le \zeta_{\mathrm{wit}}.\] Let \[I_{\mathrm{wit},n} = \int 4\eta_{\mathrm{wit}}^2h_n^{2\beta} b_\circ\!\left(\frac{t-\delta_n}{h_n}\right)^2 \,dP_{X,A}(x,t)\] be the one-observation chi-squared increment. The product law factorization gives \[1+\chi^2(Q_1^{\otimes n},Q_0^{\otimes n}) = (1+I_{\mathrm{wit},n})^n .\] The perturbation is bounded by \(1/4\), so the Bernoulli likelihood ratio is finite and square-integrable for one observation; absolute continuity and square integrability tensorize to the product laws, giving a well-posed product chi-squared divergence. Since \(I_{\mathrm{wit},n}\ge0\) and \(nI_{\mathrm{wit},n}\le\zeta_{\mathrm{wit}}\), the inequality \(1+u\le e^u\) gives \[\chi^2(Q_1^{\otimes n},Q_0^{\otimes n}) \le \exp(nI_{\mathrm{wit},n})-1 \le \exp(\zeta_{\mathrm{wit}})-1 \le D_{\mathrm{wit}} .\]

  7. Now set \[c= \frac12\min\{c_{\mathrm r},c_{\mathrm{loc}},1/2,\eta_{\mathrm{wit}}\}, \qquad C=C_{\mathrm{up}}+D_{\mathrm{wit}}+\exp(4)+c+1 .\] The amplitude asserted in the theorem is \(\eta_{\mathrm{wit}}\). Then \(0<c<C\), and \(c\le1/2\), \(c\le \eta_{\mathrm{wit}}\), \(2c\le c_{\mathrm r}\), and \(2c\le c_{\mathrm{loc}}\). Increasing the eventual index so that all preceding bounds hold, put \[r_{0,n}=n^{-1/2}, \qquad r_{1,n}=\delta_n^{\kappa+1}h_n^\beta, \qquad r_n=r_{0,n}+r_{1,n}.\] The two lower components imply \[R_n^\star\ge c_{\mathrm r}r_{0,n}, \qquad R_n^\star\ge c_{\mathrm{loc}}r_{1,n}.\] Since \(r_{0,n},r_{1,n}\ge0\), these inequalities and the choices of \(c\) give \[R_n^\star \ge \max\{2c\,r_{0,n},2c\,r_{1,n}\} \ge \frac12(2c\,r_{0,n})+ \frac12(2c\,r_{1,n}) = c r_n .\] Together with the admissibility and upper bounds above, \[c r_n \le R_n^\star \le \mathcal R_n^{\mathrm{TG}}(B_n) \le C r_n .\] By Definitions 16 and 2, \(\mathcal R_n^{\mathrm{TG}}(B_n)\) is the finite-sample worst-case risk \(\operatorname{Risk}_n(B_n)\) of the stabilized estimator computed under the observed i.i.d. product law. The global witness constructed above satisfies \[c n^{-1/2} \le \left|\theta_{\delta_n}(P_1)-\theta_{\delta_n}(P_0)\right|, \qquad \chi^2(P_1^{\otimes n},P_0^{\otimes n})\le C,\] because \(c\le1/2\) and \(\exp(4)\le C\). The localized witness constructed above satisfies \[c\delta_n^{\kappa+1}h_n^\beta \le \left|\theta_{\delta_n}(Q_1)-\theta_{\delta_n}(Q_0)\right|, \qquad \chi^2(Q_1^{\otimes n},Q_0^{\otimes n})\le C,\] because \(c\le \eta_{\mathrm{wit}}\) and \(D_{\mathrm{wit}}\le C\). These are the asserted eventual bounds and witness properties.

∎
Proof of Theorem 2.

  1. Let \(\ell=\lceil\beta\rceil-1\) and \[t_\alpha=\sqrt{\frac{\log(12J/\alpha)}{2}} .\] The expected-length upper bound used below is the following eventual statement: there is a constant \(C_{\mathrm{up}}>0\), depending only on the declared regime constants, such that for every admissible threshold path and split path, \[\sup_{P\in\mathcal M} \mathbb E_{P^{\otimes n}} \!\left[\operatorname{len}(\mathrm{CI}_n)\right] \le C_{\mathrm{up}} \left(n^{-1/2}+\delta_n^{\kappa+1}h_n^\beta\right)\] for all sufficiently large \(n\). To see this, use Algorithm 2. Intersecting the symmetric interval with \([0,1]\) weakly shortens its length, and therefore \[\mathbb E_{P^{\otimes n}}\!\left[\operatorname{len}(\mathrm{CI}_n)\right] \le 2\,\mathbb E_{P^{\otimes n}}\!\left[ b_{0,n}+\sum_{x\in\mathcal X}D_{n,x}\right],\] where \[b_{0,n}=\frac{t_\alpha}{\sqrt{|I_{0,n}|}}, \qquad b_{1,n}=\frac{t_\alpha}{\sqrt{|I_{1,n}|}},\] and \(D_{n,x}\) is the stratum contribution in Algorithm 2. The split lower bounds in Definition 1 give \(b_{0,n},b_{1,n}\le 3t_\alpha n^{-1/2}\) once \(n\ge8\).

    For a fixed law \(P\in\mathcal M\) and stratum \(x\), the integrated stratum contribution is bounded by \[\begin{aligned} \mathbb E_{P^{\otimes n}}D_{n,x} &\le \left(\mathbb E_{P^{\otimes n}}\widehat b_x+b_{1,n}\right) \left[ C_{\ell,1}Lh_n^\beta +t_\alpha \int \left(\sum_{i\in I_{2,n}}w_{ix}(z)^2\right)^{1/2} \,dP^{\otimes n}(z) \right] +b_{1,n}+P^{\otimes n}(\Omega_x^c) . \end{aligned}\] Here \(C_{\ell,1}\) is the finite \(\ell_1\)-weight constant supplied on \(\Omega_x\) by Lemma 17. The displayed product bound is the sample-splitting step: the empirical atom estimate uses \(I_{1,n}\), the local weights use \(I_{2,n}\), and the mixed term factors as \[\int \widehat b_x(z) \left(\sum_{i\in I_{2,n}}w_{ix}(z)^2\right)^{1/2} \,dP^{\otimes n}(z) = \left(\int\widehat b_x\,dP^{\otimes n}\right) \left(\int \left(\sum_{i\in I_{2,n}}w_{ix}^2\right)^{1/2} \,dP^{\otimes n}\right).\] For the empirical atom envelope, define \[f_x(O)=\mathbf 1\{X=x,\ A\leq\delta_n\}, \qquad m_x(P)=p_xq_x(\delta_n).\] Then \(\widehat b_x=|I_{1,n}|^{-1}\sum_{i\in I_{1,n}}f_x(O_i)\). The mean of one summand is \[\mathbb E_P f_x(O) =P\{X=x,A\leq\delta_n\} =p_x\int_0^{\delta_n}\pi_x(a)\,da =p_xq_x(\delta_n) =m_x(P).\] The second equality is the conditional-density component of Definition 5, and the third is the definition of \(q_x\) in Definition 7. Since \(0\leq f_x\leq1\), \(\operatorname{Var}_P\{f_x(O)\}\leq1/4\). Independence on the deterministic second block therefore yields \[\begin{aligned} \mathbb E_{P^{\otimes n}} \left|\widehat b_x-m_x(P)\right| &\leq \left\{\operatorname{Var}_{P^{\otimes n}}(\widehat b_x)\right\}^{1/2} \\ &\leq \frac12 |I_{1,n}|^{-1/2}. \end{aligned}\] Moreover, Proposition 6 gives \(q_x(\delta_n)\leq c_+\delta_n^{\kappa+1}/(\kappa+1)\), and \(p_x\leq1\), so \[m_x(P)\leq \frac{c_+}{\kappa+1}\delta_n^{\kappa+1}.\] The triangle inequality gives the integrated envelope \[\mathbb E_{P^{\otimes n}}\widehat b_x \le \frac12 |I_{1,n}|^{-1/2} + \frac{c_+}{\kappa+1}\delta_n^{\kappa+1}.\] Also, Lemma 13 gives \[\int \left(\sum_{i\in I_{2,n}}w_{ix}(z)^2\right)^{1/2} \,dP^{\otimes n}(z) \le C_{\mathrm{eng}}h_n^\beta\] for a finite constant \(C_{\mathrm{eng}}\), and Lemma 17 gives \[P^{\otimes n}(\Omega_x^c) \le C_{\mathrm{tg}}\exp\{-c_{\mathrm{tg}}n h_n(\delta_n+h_n)^\kappa\}.\] By Lemma 10, for all sufficiently large \(n\), \[n h_n^{2\beta+1}(\delta_n+h_n)^\kappa=1, \qquad 0<h_n, \qquad h_n\le1.\] The effective local sample size has the following lower bound. Put \[\gamma_{\mathrm{bal}}=2\beta+\kappa+1 .\] Since \(\delta_n\ge0\), \(h_n>0\), and \(\kappa\ge0\), \[h_n^{\gamma_{\mathrm{bal}}} =h_n^{2\beta+1}h_n^\kappa \le h_n^{2\beta+1}(\delta_n+h_n)^\kappa =n^{-1}.\] Because \(\gamma_{\mathrm{bal}}>0\), this gives \(h_n\le n^{-1/\gamma_{\mathrm{bal}}}\). Multiplying the balance identity by \(h_n^{-2\beta}\) gives \[n h_n(\delta_n+h_n)^\kappa=h_n^{-2\beta},\] and therefore, since \(-2\beta<0\), \[n^{2\beta/(2\beta+\kappa+1)} =\left(n^{-1/\gamma_{\mathrm{bal}}}\right)^{-2\beta} \le h_n^{-2\beta} =n h_n(\delta_n+h_n)^\kappa .\] Exponential decay along this polynomial scale is eventually bounded by \(n^{-1/2}\). Combining the exponential-decay bound with the polynomial scale bound for \(n h_n(\delta_n+h_n)^\kappa\), and using \(h_n^\beta\le1\), yields uniformly in \(P\in\mathcal M\) and \(x\in\mathcal X\), \[\mathbb E_{P^{\otimes n}}D_{n,x} \le C_{\mathrm{rad}} \left(n^{-1/2}+\delta_n^{\kappa+1}h_n^\beta\right).\] Since \(J\) is fixed, summing over strata gives the displayed \(C_{\mathrm{up}}\) bound.

  2. Put \(\eta=1-2\alpha>0\). The lower-bound input is a two-point inequality for expected length. Because an arbitrary interval procedure need not have finite expected length, the worst-case length in this step is taken in \([0,\infty]\), with \(\int^{-}\) denoting the lower integral of a nonnegative measurable function; for the bounded intervals of interest this is the ordinary expected length. If \(P_0,P_1\in\mathcal M\) satisfy \[\Delta\le |\theta_{\delta_n}(P_1)-\theta_{\delta_n}(P_0)|, \qquad P_1^{\otimes n}\ll P_0^{\otimes n},\] the likelihood-ratio square is \(P_0^{\otimes n}\)-integrable, and \(\chi^2(P_1^{\otimes n},P_0^{\otimes n})\le\chi\), then every uniformly honest observed-sample interval procedure \(C_n\) satisfies \[\max\!\left\{\left(1-2\alpha-\frac12\sqrt\chi\right)\Delta,0\right\} \le \sup\left\{ \int^{-}\operatorname{len}(C_n)\,dP^{\otimes n}: P\in\mathcal M \right\},\] where the supremum and the lower integral are taken in \([0,\infty]\) and the nonnegative real number on the left is read as an element of \([0,\infty]\) as in Definition 23. The comparisons in this step are made in \([0,\infty]\); the final paragraph returns to the ordinary expected-length notation of Definitions 18 and 15 once the interval is known to be bounded. Indeed, coverage at \(P_0\) and \(P_1\), transferred from \(P_1^{\otimes n}\) to \(P_0^{\otimes n}\) by \[d_{\mathrm{TV}}(P_1^{\otimes n},P_0^{\otimes n}) \le \frac12\sqrt{\chi},\] gives \(P_0^{\otimes n}\{\theta_{\delta_n}(P_0),\theta_{\delta_n}(P_1)\in C_n\}\ge 1-2\alpha-\frac12\sqrt\chi\). On that event the interval length is at least \(\Delta\), and integrating this indicator gives the displayed extended-real bound.

    For the root component, set \[\varepsilon_n=\tau_\alpha n^{-1/2}, \qquad \tau_\alpha=\frac{\eta}{16}, \qquad \chi_\alpha=\frac{\eta^2}{16}.\] Let \(\mathbb P^{\mathrm{root}}_{0,n}\) and \(\mathbb P^{\mathrm{root}}_{1,n}\) have the common design \[\mathbb P(X=x,A\in da)=\frac1J(\kappa+1)a^\kappa\mathbf 1\{0\leq a\leq1\}\,da,\] and, conditionally on \((X,A)=(x,a)\), let \(Y\) be Bernoulli with success probability \[\frac12+g^{\mathrm{root}}_{j,n}(x,a), \qquad g^{\mathrm{root}}_{0,n}(x,a)=0,\qquad g^{\mathrm{root}}_{1,n}(x,a)=\varepsilon_n .\] For all sufficiently large \(n\), \(|\varepsilon_n|\leq1/2\). The two laws therefore have outcomes in \([0,1]\), the same density \(\pi_x(a)=(\kappa+1)a^\kappa\), the same masses \(p_x=1/J\), and constant Hölder regressions; the regime inequalities place both laws in \(\mathcal M\). Their clamp-target gap is exactly \[\begin{aligned} \theta_{\delta_n}(\mathbb P^{\mathrm{root}}_{1,n}) -\theta_{\delta_n}(\mathbb P^{\mathrm{root}}_{0,n}) &=\varepsilon_n \left[ \mathbb P(A>\delta_n)+\sum_{x\in\mathcal X}p_xq_x(\delta_n) \right] \\ &=\varepsilon_n . \end{aligned}\] For one observation the chi-square divergence is \(4\varepsilon_n^2\), hence independence gives \[1+\chi^2\!\left((\mathbb P^{\mathrm{root}}_{1,n})^{\otimes n}, (\mathbb P^{\mathrm{root}}_{0,n})^{\otimes n}\right) =(1+4\varepsilon_n^2)^n.\] Since \(4n\varepsilon_n^2=\eta^2/64\), \[(1+4\varepsilon_n^2)^n \le \exp(\eta^2/64) \le 1+\eta^2/64+(\eta^2/64)^2 \le 1+\chi_\alpha .\] The two-point length inequality above, with \(\chi=\chi_\alpha\), yields \[\max\!\left\{ \left(\eta-\frac12\sqrt{\chi_\alpha}\right)\varepsilon_n,0 \right\} \le L_n^\star .\] Thus, for some \(c_{\mathrm{root}}>0\), \[\max\!\left\{c_{\mathrm{root}}n^{-1/2},0\right\} \le L_n^\star\] eventually.

    For the local component, use the fixed compactly supported \(C^\infty\) bump \(\mathfrak b:\mathbb R\to\mathbb R\) from the same two-point construction, normalized by \[\mathfrak b(0)=1, \qquad 0\leq\mathfrak b(u)\leq1, \qquad \mathfrak b(u)=0\ \text{whenever } |u|\geq1 .\] The smoothness calibration for this particular bump gives an amplitude \(\mathfrak a_{\mathrm{bump}}\in(0,1/4]\), depending only on \((\beta,L)\), with the following uniform property. For every \(0\leq\delta\leq1\) and \(0<h\leq1\), define \[q_{\delta,h}^{\mathrm{bump}}(a) = \mathfrak a_{\mathrm{bump}}h^\beta \mathfrak b\!\left(\frac{a-\delta}{h}\right).\] Then \(a\mapsto 1/2+q_{\delta,h}^{\mathrm{bump}}(a)\) is continuous on \([0,1]\), takes values in \([0,1]\), and satisfies the Hölder Taylor remainder \[\left| \left(\frac12+q_{\delta,h}^{\mathrm{bump}}(t)\right) - \sum_{j=0}^{\ell} \frac{\partial_{[0,1]}^j(1/2+q_{\delta,h}^{\mathrm{bump}})(s)}{j!}(t-s)^j \right| \le L|t-s|^\beta\] for all \(s,t\in[0,1]\). Indeed, applying the normalized Hölder gate of \(\mathfrak b\) with radius \(\min\{L,1\}\) and then shrinking the amplitude to be at most \(1/4\) gives both the displayed Taylor bound and the range constraint.

    Set \[\zeta_{\mathrm{loc}} = \frac{\eta^2/64}{8(\kappa+1)\mathfrak a_{\mathrm{bump}}^2+1}, \qquad h_{n,\mathrm{loc}}=\zeta_{\mathrm{loc}}h_n .\] Let \(\mathbb P^{\mathrm{loc}}_{0,n}\) and \(\mathbb P^{\mathrm{loc}}_{1,n}\) have the same common design as the root pair, and let their conditional Bernoulli success probabilities be \(1/2+g^{\mathrm{loc}}_{j,n}(x,a)\), where \[g^{\mathrm{loc}}_{0,n}(x,a)=0,\qquad g^{\mathrm{loc}}_{1,n}(x,a) =\mathfrak a_{\mathrm{bump}}h_{n,\mathrm{loc}}^\beta \mathfrak b\!\left(\frac{a-\delta_n}{h_{n,\mathrm{loc}}}\right).\] For all sufficiently large \(n\), Lemma 10 gives \(0<h_n\le1\), hence \(0<h_{n,\mathrm{loc}}\le1\), and the amplitude calibration places both laws in \(\mathcal M\). The localized shift is nonnegative and equals \(\mathfrak a_{\mathrm{bump}}h_{n,\mathrm{loc}}^\beta\) at \(a=\delta_n\), so the collapsed atom contribution alone gives \[\left| \theta_{\delta_n}(\mathbb P^{\mathrm{loc}}_{1,n}) -\theta_{\delta_n}(\mathbb P^{\mathrm{loc}}_{0,n}) \right| \geq \mathfrak a_{\mathrm{bump}}\delta_n^{\kappa+1}h_{n,\mathrm{loc}}^\beta = \mathfrak a_{\mathrm{bump}}\zeta_{\mathrm{loc}}^\beta \delta_n^{\kappa+1}h_n^\beta .\] For one observation, write \[\mathcal I_n = \int 4\{g^{\mathrm{loc}}_{1,n}(x,a)\}^2\,d\mathbb P_{X,A}(x,a),\] where \(\mathbb P_{X,A}\) is the common design law. Since \(0\leq\mathfrak b\leq1\) and \(\mathfrak b\) vanishes outside \([-1,1]\), \[\mathcal I_n \le 8(\kappa+1)\mathfrak a_{\mathrm{bump}}^2 h_{n,\mathrm{loc}}^{2\beta+1}(\delta_n+h_{n,\mathrm{loc}})^\kappa .\] Because \(0<\zeta_{\mathrm{loc}}\leq1\), \(h_{n,\mathrm{loc}}\leq h_n\). With \(2\beta+1>1\), \(\kappa\geq0\), and the balance identity from Lemma 10, \[\begin{aligned} n\mathcal I_n &\le 8(\kappa+1)\mathfrak a_{\mathrm{bump}}^2 \zeta_{\mathrm{loc}}^{2\beta+1} \,n h_n^{2\beta+1}(\delta_n+h_n)^\kappa \\ &\le \eta^2/64 . \end{aligned}\] Independence gives \[1+\chi^2\!\left((\mathbb P^{\mathrm{loc}}_{1,n})^{\otimes n}, (\mathbb P^{\mathrm{loc}}_{0,n})^{\otimes n}\right) =(1+\mathcal I_n)^n \le \exp(\eta^2/64) \le 1+\chi_\alpha .\] The two-point length inequality therefore gives, for some \(c_{\mathrm{loc}}>0\), \[\max\!\left\{c_{\mathrm{loc}}\delta_n^{\kappa+1}h_n^\beta,0\right\} \le L_n^\star\] eventually. Taking half the smaller of \(c_{\mathrm{root}}\) and \(c_{\mathrm{loc}}\), and splitting into the two cases according to which component is larger, gives a constant \(c_{\mathrm{min}}>0\) such that \[\max\!\left\{c_{\mathrm{min}}\left(n^{-1/2}+\delta_n^{\kappa+1}h_n^\beta\right),0\right\} \le L_n^\star\] eventually.

    The every-procedure version uses that a uniformly honest procedure is an admissible competitor in the minimax honest-length criterion. Hence there is a positive constant \(c_{\mathrm{proc}}>0\) such that every uniformly honest observed-sample interval procedure \(C_n\) satisfies \[\max\!\left\{c_{\mathrm{proc}}\left(n^{-1/2}+\delta_n^{\kappa+1}h_n^\beta\right),0\right\} \le \sup\left\{ \int^{-}\operatorname{len}(C_n)\,dP^{\otimes n}: P\in\mathcal M \right\}\] in \(\mathbb R_{\ge0}\cup\{\infty\}\).

  3. Define \[c=\frac12\min\{c_{\mathrm{min}},c_{\mathrm{proc}}\}, \qquad C=C_{\mathrm{up}}+c+1 .\] Then \(c>0\), \(c<C\), \(c\le c_{\mathrm{min}}\), \(c\le c_{\mathrm{proc}}\), and \(C_{\mathrm{up}}\le C\).

  4. Fix an admissible threshold path \((\delta_n)\) and split path \(B_\bullet\). By Lemma 10, for all sufficiently large \(n\), \[0<h_n, \qquad h_n\le 1-\bar\delta, \qquad n h_n^{2\beta+1}(\delta_n+h_n)^\kappa=1 .\] Together with \(0\le\delta_n\le\bar\delta\), this gives \(\delta_n+h_n\le1\). Hence the hypotheses of Lemma 14 hold. That result supplies observed-sample measurability of \(\mathrm{CI}_n\) and the modelwise coverage inequality \[1-\alpha \le P^{\otimes n}\{\theta_{\delta_n}(P)\in \mathrm{CI}_n\}\] for every \(P\in\mathcal M\), so the stabilized interval is uniformly honest. Taking the infimum over \(P\) gives the displayed worst-case coverage bound.

  5. Put \[r_n=n^{-1/2}+\delta_n^{\kappa+1}h_n^\beta .\] Since \(n^{-1/2}>0\) and \(\delta_n^{\kappa+1}h_n^\beta\ge0\), we have \(r_n>0\). The upper ingredient and \(C_{\mathrm{up}}\le C\) yield \[\sup_{P\in\mathcal M} \mathbb E_{P^{\otimes n}}\!\left[\operatorname{len}(\mathrm{CI}_n)\right] \le C r_n .\] The minimax lower ingredient and \(c\le c_{\mathrm{min}}\) yield the extended-real comparison \[\max\{c r_n,0\}\le L_n^\star .\]

    Since \(\mathrm{CI}_n\) is itself uniformly honest by the coverage step, the procedure lower bound applies to this concrete interval. Combined with \(c\le c_{\mathrm{proc}}\), it gives \[\max\{c r_n,0\} \le \sup\left\{ \int^{-}\operatorname{len}(\mathrm{CI}_n)\,dP^{\otimes n}: P\in\mathcal M \right\}\] in \([0,\infty]\). For the concrete stabilized interval the length is measurable, nonnegative, and bounded by one, so its worst-case length in \([0,\infty]\) agrees with its ordinary worst-case expected length, read in \([0,\infty]\) as in Definition 23. Since \(c r_n>0\), this gives the real lower bound \[c r_n \le \sup_{P\in\mathcal M} \mathbb E_{P^{\otimes n}}\!\left[\operatorname{len}(\mathrm{CI}_n)\right].\]

    Finally, for every uniformly honest observed-sample interval procedure \(C_n\), the procedure lower bound gives the extended-real statement \[\max\{c r_n,0\} \le \sup\left\{ \int^{-}\operatorname{len}(C_n)\,dP^{\otimes n}: P\in\mathcal M \right\},\] with the supremum taken in \(\mathbb R_{\ge0}\cup\{\infty\}\). This is the arbitrary-procedure worst-length criterion used in the comparison. For interval procedures whose pointwise lengths lie in \([0,1]\) as in Definition 15, the nonnegative lower integral equals the ordinary expected length under the positive-part embedding of Definition 23, and the display specializes to the corresponding real expected-length lower bound. These are the eventual length comparisons established by the argument.

∎
Proof of Theorem 3.

Let \[\Gamma=\beta\kappa+2\beta+\kappa+1, \qquad \Xi=2\beta+\kappa+1, \qquad \zeta=\frac{\Gamma}{2\beta+1}.\] The regime assumptions give \(\Gamma>0\), \(\Xi>0\), and \(\zeta>0\).

  1. The two deterministic scales satisfy \[\frac{\delta_{\mathrm{crit},n}}{\delta_{\mathrm{edge},n}} = \frac{n^{-1/(2\Gamma)}}{n^{-1/\Xi}} = n^{1/\Xi-1/(2\Gamma)}\] for every \(n\ge1\). The exponent is positive because \[2\Gamma-\Xi = 2\beta\kappa+2\beta+\kappa+1>0.\] Hence \(n^{1/\Xi-1/(2\Gamma)}\to\infty\), which proves \[\frac{\delta_{\mathrm{crit},n}}{\delta_{\mathrm{edge},n}}\to\infty .\]

  2. Fix a deterministic threshold sequence \((\delta_n)\) with \(\delta_n\in[0,\bar\delta]\). Let \(h_n\) be the information-balance bandwidth from Definition 8, define \[a_n=\delta_n^{\kappa+1}h_n^\beta,\] and let \(r_n\) be the frontier rate in Definition 9, so that \(r_n=n^{-1/2}+a_n\). By Lemma 10, for all sufficiently large \(n\), \[h_n>0, \qquad h_n\le 1-\bar\delta, \qquad n h_n^{2\beta+1}(\delta_n+h_n)^\kappa=1 .\] The phase comparisons below use this deterministic balance relation directly. In the regular regime \(\delta_n/\delta_{\mathrm{crit},n}\to0\), we also have, for all sufficiently large \(n\), \[\frac{\delta_n}{\delta_{\mathrm{crit},n}}<1 .\] If \(\delta_n=0\), then \(a_n=0\) because \(\kappa+1>0\). If \(\delta_n>0\), set \[\eta_n=\bigl(n\delta_n^\kappa\bigr)^{-1/(2\beta+1)} .\] Since \(\delta_n+h_n\ge\delta_n\) and \(\kappa\ge0\), the balance equation implies \[h_n\le \eta_n .\] Therefore \[a_n = \delta_n^{\kappa+1}h_n^\beta \le \delta_n^{\kappa+1}\eta_n^\beta .\] The exact power computation is \[\frac{\delta_n^{\kappa+1}\eta_n^\beta}{n^{-1/2}} = \left(\frac{\delta_n}{\delta_{\mathrm{crit},n}}\right)^\zeta \le 1 .\] Thus \(0\le a_n\le n^{-1/2}\) eventually, and \[n^{-1/2}\le r_n=n^{-1/2}+a_n\le 2n^{-1/2}\] eventually. Hence \(r_n\asymp n^{-1/2}\).

  3. Suppose \(\delta_n/\delta_{\mathrm{crit},n}\to c_0\) with \(c_0>0\). By the scale separation \(\delta_{\mathrm{crit},n}/\delta_{\mathrm{edge},n}\to\infty\), \[\frac{\delta_n}{\delta_{\mathrm{edge},n}} = \frac{\delta_n}{\delta_{\mathrm{crit},n}} \frac{\delta_{\mathrm{crit},n}}{\delta_{\mathrm{edge},n}} \to\infty .\] For this above-edge regime, the balance equation gives the closed-form bandwidth order. Indeed, for all sufficiently large \(n\), \(\delta_n>0\) and \(\delta_{\mathrm{edge},n}\le\delta_n\). With \(\eta_n=(n\delta_n^\kappa)^{-1/(2\beta+1)}\), the balance equation gives \(h_n\le \eta_n\). The inequality \(\delta_{\mathrm{edge},n}\le\delta_n\) gives \(\eta_n\le\delta_n\), and hence \(\delta_n+h_n\le2\delta_n\). Substituting this upper bound into the same balance equation yields \[2^{-\kappa/(2\beta+1)} \eta_n\le h_n\le \eta_n .\] Consequently \[h_n\asymp \bigl(n\delta_n^\kappa\bigr)^{-1/(2\beta+1)} .\] Multiplying by the nonnegative factor \(\delta_n^{\kappa+1}\) and raising the bandwidth comparison to the positive power \(\beta\) gives \[a_n\asymp \delta_n^{\kappa+1} \left\{\bigl(n\delta_n^\kappa\bigr)^{-1/(2\beta+1)}\right\}^{\beta}.\] The normalized interior term satisfies \[\frac{ \delta_n^{\kappa+1} \left\{\bigl(n\delta_n^\kappa\bigr)^{-1/(2\beta+1)}\right\}^{\beta} }{n^{-1/2}} = \left(\frac{\delta_n}{\delta_{\mathrm{crit},n}}\right)^\zeta \to c_0^\zeta\in(0,\infty).\] Consequently \(a_n\asymp n^{-1/2}\). Since \(r_n=n^{-1/2}+a_n\) and both summands are nonnegative, also \[r_n\asymp n^{-1/2}.\]

  4. Suppose \(\delta_n/\delta_{\mathrm{crit},n}\to\infty\) and \(\delta_n\to0\). The scale separation \(\delta_{\mathrm{crit},n}/\delta_{\mathrm{edge},n}\to\infty\) again yields \[\frac{\delta_n}{\delta_{\mathrm{edge},n}}\to\infty,\] so the same direct balance comparison used at the critical scale gives \[h_n\asymp \bigl(n\delta_n^\kappa\bigr)^{-1/(2\beta+1)} .\] Thus \[a_n\asymp \delta_n^{\kappa+1} \bigl(n\delta_n^\kappa\bigr)^{-\beta/(2\beta+1)} .\] Moreover, \[\frac{ \delta_n^{\kappa+1} \bigl(n\delta_n^\kappa\bigr)^{-\beta/(2\beta+1)} }{n^{-1/2}} = \left(\frac{\delta_n}{\delta_{\mathrm{crit},n}}\right)^\zeta \to\infty .\] Hence the root term is eventually bounded by a constant multiple of the displayed atom scale, and \[r_n = n^{-1/2}+a_n \asymp \delta_n^{\kappa+1} \bigl(n\delta_n^\kappa\bigr)^{-\beta/(2\beta+1)} .\]

  5. Suppose \(\delta_n\to\delta_0\) with \(\delta_0\in(0,\bar\delta]\). Since \[\delta_{\mathrm{crit},n}=n^{-1/(2\Gamma)}\to0,\] we have \[\frac{\delta_n}{\delta_{\mathrm{crit},n}}\to\infty .\] Together with the scale separation \(\delta_{\mathrm{crit},n}/\delta_{\mathrm{edge},n}\to\infty\), this places the sequence above the edge scale. The same direct balance comparison used in the supercritical case gives \[r_n\asymp \delta_n^{\kappa+1} \bigl(n\delta_n^\kappa\bigr)^{-\beta/(2\beta+1)} .\] For all sufficiently large \(n\), \(\delta_n>0\), and \[\delta_n^{\kappa+1} \bigl(n\delta_n^\kappa\bigr)^{-\beta/(2\beta+1)} = n^{-\beta/(2\beta+1)}\delta_n^\zeta .\] Because \(\delta_n^\zeta\to\delta_0^\zeta\in(0,\infty)\), it follows that \[r_n\asymp n^{-\beta/(2\beta+1)} .\]

  6. Fix \(n\) and \(P\in\mathcal M\). At threshold \(0\), Definition 7 gives, for every stratum \(x\), \[q_x(0)=\int_{[0,0]}\pi_x(a)\,\mathrm da=0 .\] The conditional-density condition in Assumption 2 gives \[P(A=0,X=x)=p_x\int_{\{0\}}\pi_x(a)\,\mathrm da=0\] for every stratum \(x\). Summing over the finite stratum set yields \(P(A=0)=0\), and the treatment support encoded by Definition 5 gives \(A>0\) almost surely. Therefore the retained term in Definition 7 satisfies \[\nu_0(P) = \int Y\,\mathbf 1\{A>0\}\,dP = \int Y\,dP .\] Since every \(q_x(0)\) is zero, \[\theta_0(P) = \nu_0(P)+\sum_x p_x q_x(0)\mu_x^P(0) = \int Y\,dP .\] Finally, \(\kappa+1>0\), so Definition 9 gives \[r_n\big|_{\delta_n=0} = n^{-1/2}+0^{\kappa+1}h_n^\beta = n^{-1/2}.\]

  7. Fix \(n\), \(P\in\mathcal M\), and an admissible split \(B=(I_0,I_1,I_2)\) as in Definition 1. The conditional-density condition in Assumption 2 gives \(P(A=0)=0\), so \(A>0\) under \(P\) almost surely. Since the product law in Definition 2 has each observed coordinate distributed as \(P\), every coordinate satisfies \(A_i>0\) under \(P^{\otimes n}\) almost surely, and the finite intersection of these coordinate events still has probability one. On that event, the finite-block average is interpreted as the totalized expression \[\operatorname{Avg}_I(Y) = \begin{cases} |I|^{-1}\sum_{i\in I}Y_i,& |I|>0,\\ 0,& |I|=0, \end{cases}\] which is the displayed product \((|I|)^{-1}\sum_{i\in I}Y_i\) with the real-inverse convention \(0^{-1}=0\). Thus \[\widehat b_x = |I_1|^{-1}\sum_{i\in I_1}\mathbf 1\{X_i=x,\ A_i\le0\} = 0 \qquad\text{for every }x,\] while \[\widehat\nu_n = |I_0|^{-1}\sum_{i\in I_0}Y_i .\] Substituting these identities into Algorithm 1 gives, \(P^{\otimes n}\)-almost surely, \[\widehat\theta_n = \Pi_{[0,1]} \left( |I_0|^{-1}\sum_{i\in I_0}Y_i + \sum_x 0\cdot \widetilde\mu_x \right) = \Pi_{[0,1]} \left( |I_0|^{-1}\sum_{i\in I_0}Y_i \right).\] Thus the threshold-zero total-Gram estimator agrees under the product sampling law with the clamped block-zero outcome average.

  8. Apply Theorem 2 to the deterministic sequence \(\delta_n\equiv0\) and to a deterministic admissible split sequence \(B_\bullet=(B_n)_{n\ge1}\). The identity \(r_n=n^{-1/2}\) at threshold zero gives eventual worst-case coverage \[\inf_{P\in\mathcal M} P^{\otimes n}\! \left\{\theta_0(P)\in \mathrm{CI}_{n,B_n}\right\} \ge 1-\alpha,\] and constants \(0<c_{\mathrm{len}}\le C_{\mathrm{len}}<\infty\) such that, eventually, the concrete stabilized length from Definition 16 satisfies \[c_{\mathrm{len}}\,n^{-1/2} \le \operatorname{Length}_n(B_n) \le C_{\mathrm{len}}\,n^{-1/2}.\] Likewise, applying Theorem 1 to the same zero sequence and using \(r_n=n^{-1/2}\) gives constants \(0<c_{\mathrm{risk}}\le C_{\mathrm{risk}}<\infty\) such that, eventually, the concrete stabilized risk from Definition 16 satisfies \[c_{\mathrm{risk}}\,n^{-1/2} \le \operatorname{Risk}_n(B_n) \le C_{\mathrm{risk}}\,n^{-1/2}.\] Therefore the stabilized zero-threshold procedure has coverage at least \(1-\alpha\) for all sufficiently large \(n\), and its stabilized worst-case absolute-error risk and stabilized worst-case expected length at threshold \(0\) are both \(\asymp n^{-1/2}\).

∎
Proof of Proposition 1.

  1. Put \[\tau_{\delta,h}(a)=\max\{0,1-|a-\delta|/h\}, \qquad \gamma_{\delta,h}(a)=h\,\tau_{\delta,h}(a),\] so that, on the eventual range where \(h>0\), \[m_0(a)=\frac12, \qquad m_{1,\delta,h}(a)=\frac12+\gamma_{\delta,h}(a), \qquad \pi(a)=2a .\] Let \[\zeta_n=((n)\delta_n)^{-1/3}.\] For the infimum construction in Definition 8, set \[F_n(h)=n h^3(\delta_n+h).\] The bounds \(0\le \delta_n\le\bar\delta\) and \(0<\bar\delta<1\) make \(F_n\) continuous and strictly increasing on \([0,1-\bar\delta]\), with \(F_n(0)=0\). For all sufficiently large \(n\), \(F_n(1-\bar\delta)\ge1\), so the crossing set in Definition 8 is a nonempty interval whose left endpoint is the positive solution of \(F_n(h)=1\). Hence, for all sufficiently large \(n\), \[0<h_n\le 1-\bar\delta, \qquad n h_n^3(\delta_n+h_n)=1 .\] Since \(\delta_n/n^{-1/4}\to\infty\), eventually \(n^{-1/4}\le \delta_n\), hence \(0<\delta_n\) and \[n\delta_n^4\ge 1 .\] The balance equation gives \[1=n h_n^3(\delta_n+h_n)\ge n h_n^3\delta_n,\] and therefore \(h_n\le \zeta_n\). The inequality \(n\delta_n^4\ge1\) is exactly \[\zeta_n=(n\delta_n)^{-1/3}\le \delta_n .\] Consequently \(h_n\le\delta_n\), and then \[1=n h_n^3(\delta_n+h_n)\le 2n\delta_n h_n^3,\] so \[2^{-1/3}\zeta_n\le h_n\le\zeta_n\] eventually. Also \(\delta_n+h_n\le\bar\delta+(1-\bar\delta)=1\), and the same balance equation forces \(h_n\le1/4\) eventually, since \(h_n>1/4\) and \(n\ge257\) would imply \[1=n h_n^3(\delta_n+h_n)\ge n h_n^4>1 .\] Thus \[h_n\asymp (n\delta_n)^{-1/3}.\] This is the one-stratum \(\beta=\kappa=1\) far-edge instance of the bandwidth comparison in Lemma 16, specialized to the density \(\pi(a)=2a\) and the condition \(\delta_n/n^{-1/4}\to\infty\).

  2. For all sufficiently large \(n\), the support of \(\gamma_{\delta_n,h_n}\) is contained in \([\delta_n-h_n,\delta_n+h_n]\subset[0,1]\). On the left and right halves of this interval, \[\gamma_{\delta_n,h_n}(a)=a-\delta_n+h_n \quad(\delta_n-h_n\le a\le\delta_n),\] and \[\gamma_{\delta_n,h_n}(a)=\delta_n+h_n-a \quad(\delta_n\le a\le\delta_n+h_n).\] Hence \[\begin{aligned} \int_0^1 \gamma_{\delta_n,h_n}(a)^2\,2a\,da &= \int_0^{h_n}2(\delta_n-u)(h_n-u)^2\,du +\int_0^{h_n}2(\delta_n+u)(h_n-u)^2\,du \\ &= 4\delta_n\int_0^{h_n}(h_n-u)^2\,du = \frac43\,\delta_n h_n^3 . \end{aligned}\]

  3. Because \(0\le\tau_{\delta_n,h_n}\le1\) and \(h_n\le1/4\) eventually, \[|\gamma_{\delta_n,h_n}(a)|\le\frac14 \qquad (0\le a\le1).\] For every real \(g\) with \(|g|\le1/4\), the Bernoulli comparison used here is \[2g^2 \le \operatorname{KL}\!\left(\operatorname{Bernoulli}\!\left(\frac12+g\right), \operatorname{Bernoulli}\!\left(\frac12\right)\right) \le 4g^2 .\] The lower bound follows from Pinsker applied to the one-point event \(\{1\}\), and the upper bound follows from the exact Bernoulli KL formula on the interval \([1/4,3/4]\). Integrating this display against the nonnegative density \(2a\) and using the local perturbation-energy computation gives \[\frac83\,n\delta_n h_n^3 \le K_n \le \frac{16}{3}\,n\delta_n h_n^3\] eventually. Therefore \[K_n\asymp n\delta_n h_n^3 .\]

  4. The collapsed atom mass is \[\int_0^{\delta_n}2a\,da=\delta_n^2,\] and \(\gamma_{\delta_n,h_n}(\delta_n)=h_n\). The retained-course contribution is supported on \((\delta_n,\delta_n+h_n]\), so \[\begin{aligned} \int_{\delta_n}^1 \gamma_{\delta_n,h_n}(a)\,2a\,da &= \int_{\delta_n}^{\delta_n+h_n} (\delta_n+h_n-a)\,2a\,da \\ &= \int_0^{h_n}2(\delta_n+u)(h_n-u)\,du = \delta_n h_n^2+\frac13 h_n^3 . \end{aligned}\] Thus \[\Delta_n = \delta_n^2h_n+\delta_nh_n^2+\frac13 h_n^3 .\] Since \(0<h_n\le\delta_n\) eventually, \[\delta_n^2h_n \le \Delta_n \le \frac73\,\delta_n^2h_n\] eventually, and hence \[\Delta_n\asymp \delta_n^2h_n .\]

  5. Let \[\chi_n=n^{-1/10}, \qquad \varrho_n=n^{-1/2}, \qquad \upsilon_n=\delta_n^2\zeta_n =\delta_n^2(n\delta_n)^{-1/3}.\] For all sufficiently large \(n\), \(\delta_n>0\), and direct exponent algebra gives \[\frac{\upsilon_n}{\varrho_n} = \left(\frac{\delta_n}{\chi_n}\right)^{5/3}.\] Combining the bandwidth comparison from the first item with the target comparison from the fourth item gives constants \(\underline{\mathfrak h},\overline{\mathfrak h}>0\) such that eventually \[\underline{\mathfrak h}\,\upsilon_n \le \Delta_n \le \overline{\mathfrak h}\,\upsilon_n .\] If \(\delta_n\asymp\chi_n\), then for some \(\underline{\mathfrak d},\overline{\mathfrak d}>0\), \[\underline{\mathfrak d} \le \frac{\delta_n}{\chi_n} \le \overline{\mathfrak d}\] eventually. Since \(x\mapsto x^{5/3}\) is increasing on \([0,\infty)\), \[\underline{\mathfrak h}\,\underline{\mathfrak d}^{5/3}\varrho_n \le \Delta_n \le \overline{\mathfrak h}\,\overline{\mathfrak d}^{5/3}\varrho_n\] eventually, so \(\Delta_n\asymp n^{-1/2}\).

    Conversely, assume \(\Delta_n\asymp\varrho_n\). Then for some \(\underline{\mathfrak s},\overline{\mathfrak s}>0\), \[\underline{\mathfrak s}\varrho_n \le \Delta_n \le \overline{\mathfrak s}\varrho_n\] eventually. With \[\lambda_{\mathrm{lo}} = \left(\frac{\underline{\mathfrak s}}{\overline{\mathfrak h}}\right)^{3/5}, \qquad \lambda_{\mathrm{hi}} = \left(\frac{\overline{\mathfrak s}}{\underline{\mathfrak h}}\right)^{3/5},\] the two-sided display for \(\Delta_n\), together with \(\upsilon_n/\varrho_n=(\delta_n/\chi_n)^{5/3}\), yields \[\lambda_{\mathrm{lo}} \le \frac{\delta_n}{\chi_n} \le \lambda_{\mathrm{hi}}\] eventually. Hence \[\lambda_{\mathrm{lo}}\chi_n \le \delta_n \le (\lambda_{\mathrm{lo}}+\lambda_{\mathrm{hi}})\chi_n\] eventually, which is \(\delta_n\asymp n^{-1/10}\). This proves \[\delta_n\asymp n^{-1/10} \quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad \Delta_n\asymp n^{-1/2}.\]

∎
Proof of Proposition 2.

Stratum product identity and the cell formula. Fix \(x\in\mathcal X\). From the regime constants and Definition 20, \[p_x=P(X=x)=P^{\mathrm F}(X=x)\ge p_{\min}>0, \qquad 0\le \kappa,\qquad 0\le c_+,\qquad \bar\delta\le 1 .\] Let \(\eta_x\) be the latent-coordinate marginal of the normalized full-data stratum law \(p_x^{-1}P^{\mathrm F}|_{\{X=x\}}\). The exchangeability member of Definition 20, together with Assumption 2 for the observed margin, gives the product formula \[p_x^{-1}P^{\mathrm F}\{X=x,\ A\in da,\ U\in du\} = \pi_x(a)\mathbf 1_{[0,1]}(a)\,da\,\eta_x(du).\] Consequently, for every Borel \(B\subseteq[0,1]\), consistency on the common full-data event gives \[\begin{aligned} p_x^{-1}\int_{\{X=x,\ A\in B\}}Y\,dP^{\mathrm F} &= \int_B\int_{\mathcal U}g_x(a,u)\,\eta_x(du)\,\pi_x(a)\,da \\ &= \int_B m_x^{\mathrm F}(a)\pi_x(a)\,da . \end{aligned}\] The measurability, boundedness, and restriction-to-\([0,1]\) bookkeeping in this calculation is supplied by the jointly measurable structural response, the simultaneous consistency event, and the bounded \([0,1]\)-valued response range in Definition 20.

Pointwise identification of the threshold-range regression. For every Borel \(B_\circ\subseteq[0,\bar\delta]\), the stratum product formula for \(p_x^{-1}P^{\mathrm F}\) established for this stratum and equality of the observed margin give \[\int_{B_\circ}m_x^{\mathrm F}(a)\pi_x(a)\,da = p_x^{-1}\int_{\{X=x,\ A\in B_\circ\}}Y\,dP = \int_{B_\circ}\mu_x^P(a)\pi_x(a)\,da,\] where the second equality is the conditional-expectation version property in Assumption 5 combined with Assumption 2. The products are integrable on \([0,\bar\delta]\): both regression curves are continuous there, and the polynomial envelope in Assumption 4 bounds \(\pi_x(a)\) by \(c_+a^\kappa\) on \([0,1]\). Hence \[\int_{B_\circ}\{m_x^{\mathrm F}(a)-\mu_x^P(a)\}\pi_x(a)\,da=0 \qquad \text{for all Borel }B_\circ\subseteq[0,\bar\delta].\] Since \(c_->0\) and \(\kappa\ge0\), the same thinning envelope gives \[\pi_x(a)>0 \qquad\text{for Lebesgue-a.e. }a\in(0,\bar\delta].\] Thus \(m_x^{\mathrm F}=\mu_x^P\) Lebesgue-a.e. on \((0,\bar\delta]\). Both functions are continuous on \([0,\bar\delta]\), so equality extends to every \(a\in[0,\bar\delta]\): \[m_x^{\mathrm F}(a)=\mu_x^P(a).\] This also covers the endpoint \(a=0\) by right-continuity on the closed threshold interval.

Support preservation. Fix \(\delta\in[0,\bar\delta]\). The observed margin \(P\) is in \(\mathcal M\), and the regime constants satisfy the hypotheses of Proposition 7. Therefore, for each \(x\in\mathcal X\), \[d_\delta(a)\in \operatorname{supp}\{\pi_x(a)\,da\text{ on }[0,1]\} \qquad \text{for conditional-treatment-a.e. }a .\]

Pathwise clamp decomposition. Because \(\delta\in[0,\bar\delta]\subseteq[0,1]\), the common consistency event applies both at \(A\) and at \(\delta\). On that event, if \(\delta<A\), then \(d_\delta(A)=A\) and \(Y(d_\delta(A))=Y\); if \(A\le\delta\), then \(d_\delta(A)=\delta\) and \(Y(d_\delta(A))=Y(\delta)\). Hence \[Y(d_\delta(A)) = Y\mathbf 1\{\delta<A\} + Y(\delta)\mathbf 1\{A\le\delta\} \qquad P^{\mathrm F}\text{-a.s.}\]

The atom contribution. Define, for a full-data state \(\omega\), \[\varphi_\delta^{> }(\omega) = Y(\omega)\mathbf 1\{\delta<A(\omega)\}, \qquad \varphi_\delta^{\le }(\omega) = Y(\delta)(\omega)\mathbf 1\{A(\omega)\le\delta\}.\] The bounded outcome and potential-outcome range make these integrable. The observed-margin identity gives \[\int \varphi_\delta^{>}\,dP^{\mathrm F} = \int Y\mathbf 1\{\delta<A\}\,dP = \nu_\delta(P).\] For the lower-tail term, partition over the finite strata and use the stratum product formula for \(p_x^{-1}P^{\mathrm F}\) at the fixed dose \(\delta\): \[\begin{aligned} \int \varphi_\delta^{\le}\,dP^{\mathrm F} &= \sum_{x\in\mathcal X} \int_{\{X=x,\ A\le\delta\}}Y(\delta)\,dP^{\mathrm F} \\ &= \sum_{x\in\mathcal X} p_x\left(\int_0^\delta \pi_x(a)\,da\right)m_x^{\mathrm F}(\delta) \\ &= \sum_{x\in\mathcal X}p_xq_x(\delta)m_x^{\mathrm F}(\delta). \end{aligned}\]

Target equality. Integrating the pathwise clamp decomposition of \(Y(d_\delta(A))\) and substituting the two integrated clamp contributions \(\int\varphi_\delta^{>}\,dP^{\mathrm F}\) and \(\int\varphi_\delta^{\le}\,dP^{\mathrm F}\) yields \[\psi_\delta(P^{\mathrm F}) = \nu_\delta(P)+ \sum_{x\in\mathcal X}p_xq_x(\delta)m_x^{\mathrm F}(\delta).\] Since \(\delta\in[0,\bar\delta]\), the identification \(m_x^{\mathrm F}=\mu_x^P\) on \([0,\bar\delta]\) gives \(m_x^{\mathrm F}(\delta)=\mu_x^P(\delta)\) for every stratum, and therefore \[\psi_\delta(P^{\mathrm F}) = \nu_\delta(P)+ \sum_{x\in\mathcal X}p_xq_x(\delta)\mu_x^P(\delta) = \theta_\delta(P).\] All endpoint cases in the closed range \([0,\bar\delta]\), including \(\delta=0\) and \(\delta=\bar\delta\), are covered by the same closed-interval hypotheses used above.

∎
Proof of Proposition 3.

  1. The regime assumptions give \(J>0\), \(p_{\min}>0\), and \(\bar\delta\le 1\). Fix \(P\in\mathcal M\). Define the total regression extension \[m(x,a)= \begin{cases} \mu_x^P(a), & a\in[0,1],\\ 0, & a\notin[0,1]. \end{cases}\] The model assumptions in Definition 5 give that \(m\) is Borel measurable, \(m(x,a)\in[0,1]\) for every \((x,a)\), and \[\mathbb E_P[Y\mid X,A]=m(X,A)\qquad P\text{-a.s.}\] They also give continuity of \(a\mapsto m(x,a)\) on \([0,\bar\delta]\) for every \(x\), since \(\bar\delta\le1\), and positivity of every stratum mass: \[0<p_{\min}\le p_x=P\{X=x\}.\]

    Let \(\zeta=(x,a)\in\mathcal X\times\mathbb R\), let \[\mathsf Z(o)=(o_X,o_A), \qquad \nu=P\circ\mathsf Z^{-1},\] By Kallenberg (2002), applied to the standard Borel design and outcome spaces, let \(K^{\mathrm{raw}}_\zeta\) be a conditional-distribution kernel of \(Y\) given \(\mathsf Z=\zeta\), so that for all Borel \(\mathsf B\subseteq\mathcal X\times\mathbb R\) and \(\mathsf C\subseteq\mathbb R\), \[P\{\mathsf Z\in\mathsf B,\ Y\in\mathsf C\} = \int_{\mathsf B}K^{\mathrm{raw}}_\zeta(\mathsf C)\,d\nu(\zeta).\] Put \[\tau(y)=\min\{1,\max\{0,y\}\}, \qquad K^0_\zeta=K^{\mathrm{raw}}_\zeta\circ\tau^{-1}, \qquad \eta(\zeta)=\int y\,K^0_\zeta(dy).\] Since \(Y\in[0,1]\) \(P\)-a.s., \[K^{\mathrm{raw}}_\zeta=K^0_\zeta \quad\text{for }\nu\text{-a.e. }\zeta,\] and the conditional-expectation identity gives \[\eta(\mathsf Z)=m(\mathsf Z)\qquad P\text{-a.s.},\] equivalently \(\eta=m\) \(\nu\)-a.e. Hence the Borel set \[E=\{\zeta:\eta(\zeta)\ne m(\zeta)\}\] satisfies \(\nu(E)=0\). Define the repaired kernel \[K^\star_\zeta= \begin{cases} \delta_{m(\zeta)}, & \zeta\in E,\\ K^0_\zeta, & \zeta\in E^c. \end{cases}\] Then \(K^\star_\zeta\) is a probability law supported on \([0,1]\) for every \(\zeta\), \(K^\star=K^{\mathrm{raw}}\) \(\nu\)-a.e., and \[\int y\,K^\star_\zeta(dy)=m(\zeta)\qquad\text{for every }\zeta.\]

    Let \(\lambda_{[0,1]}\) be Lebesgue probability measure on \([0,1]\). Realize the repaired kernel by its generalized inverse: for every \(\zeta\) and \(\upsilon\in[0,1]\), let \(\mathfrak q(\zeta,\upsilon)\in[0,1]\) be \[\mathfrak q(\zeta,\upsilon) = \sup\Bigl(\{0\}\cup \{s\in[0,1]:K^\star_\zeta([0,s])<\upsilon\}\Bigr).\] The strict superlevel sets of this supremum are countable unions over rational \(s\in[0,1]\), so \((\zeta,\upsilon)\mapsto\mathfrak q(\zeta,\upsilon)\) is Borel. The right-continuity of \(t\mapsto K^\star_\zeta((-\infty,t])\) gives the generalized-inverse identity, for every \(t\in\mathbb R\), \[\lambda_{[0,1]}\{\upsilon:\mathfrak q(\zeta,\upsilon)\le t\} = K^\star_\zeta((-\infty,t])\] Therefore \[\mathfrak q(\zeta,\cdot)_\#\lambda_{[0,1]}=K^\star_\zeta.\]

    On the product space \[(\mathcal X\times\mathbb R)\times[0,1], \qquad \nu\otimes\lambda_{[0,1]},\] define \[g_x(a,\upsilon)=\mathfrak q((x,a),\upsilon), \qquad Y(a)=g_X(a,U), \qquad Y=g_X(A,U).\] Because \(K^\star=K^{\mathrm{raw}}\) \(\nu\)-a.e., the law of \((X,A,Y)\) is exactly \(P\). The displayed structural identities \(Y(a)=g_X(a,U)\) and \(Y=g_X(A,U)\) also give the simultaneous consistency condition in Definition 20. For bounded Borel \(\varphi:\mathbb R\to\mathbb R\) and \(\chi:[0,1]\to\mathbb R\), the product construction yields, for every \(x\), \[p_x \int_{\{X=x\}}\varphi(A)\chi(U)\,dP^{\mathrm F} = \left(\int_{\{X=x\}}\varphi(A)\,dP^{\mathrm F}\right) \left(\int_{\{X=x\}}\chi(U)\,dP^{\mathrm F}\right),\] which is the exchangeability condition in Definition 20. Finally, for \(a\in[0,\bar\delta]\), \[m_x^{\mathrm F}(a) = p_x^{-1}\int_{\{X=x\}}g_x(a,U)\,dP^{\mathrm F} = \int_0^1\mathfrak q((x,a),\upsilon)\,d\lambda_{[0,1]}(\upsilon) = \int y\,K^\star_{(x,a)}(dy) = m(x,a) = \mu_x^P(a).\] Thus \(a\mapsto m_x^{\mathrm F}(a)\) is continuous on \([0,\bar\delta]\). For the remainder of the proof, write \[\Gamma(P^{\mathrm F}) := \text{the observed }(X,A,Y)\text{-margin of the full-data law package }P^{\mathrm F}.\] The constructed package \(P^{\mathrm F}\) belongs to \(\mathcal M^{\mathrm F}\), and its observed margin is \(P\). Hence \[\forall P\in\mathcal M\quad \exists P^{\mathrm F}\in\mathcal M^{\mathrm F} \quad\text{with}\quad \Gamma(P^{\mathrm F})=P.\] This proves observed-margin surjectivity.

  2. Fix \(n\in\mathbb N\), including the empty-sample case \(n=0\), and fix \(\delta\in[0,\bar\delta]\). For each \(P^{\mathrm F}\in\mathcal M^{\mathrm F}\), Proposition 2 gives the target identity \[\psi_\delta(P^{\mathrm F}) = \theta_\delta\{\Gamma(P^{\mathrm F})\}.\] The causal criteria in Definition 22 evaluate every observed-sample estimator, coverage event, and length objective under the product law \(\Gamma(P^{\mathrm F})^{\otimes n}\) of the observed margin, in the notation of Definition 2. Hence, after the displayed target identity, each causal risk, coverage probability, and expected length depends on \(P^{\mathrm F}\) only through \(\Gamma(P^{\mathrm F})\).

  3. Let \(T_n\) be any observed-measurable estimator with values in \([0,1]\). The target and sampling-law identity \(\psi_\delta(P^{\mathrm F})=\theta_\delta\{\Gamma(P^{\mathrm F})\}\) gives \[\int \left|T_n(z)-\psi_\delta(P^{\mathrm F})\right|\,\mathrm d\Gamma(P^{\mathrm F})^{\otimes n}(z) = \int \left|T_n(z)-\theta_\delta\{\Gamma(P^{\mathrm F})\}\right|\,\mathrm d\Gamma(P^{\mathrm F})^{\otimes n}(z).\] Taking suprema and using the surjectivity already proved, \[\sup_{P^{\mathrm F}\in\mathcal M^{\mathrm F}} \int \left|T_n(z)-\psi_\delta(P^{\mathrm F})\right|\,\mathrm d\Gamma(P^{\mathrm F})^{\otimes n}(z) = \sup_{P\in\mathcal M} \int \left|T_n(z)-\theta_\delta(P)\right|\,\mathrm dP^{\otimes n}(z).\] The admissible estimator class is the same on both sides, so taking the infimum over \(T_n\) and using Definitions 22 and 18 gives \[R_{n,\mathrm F}^{\star}(\delta)=R_n^\star(\delta).\]

  4. Let \(\mathcal C_n\) be an observed-measurable interval procedure. The same target and product-law identities give \[\Gamma(P^{\mathrm F})^{\otimes n} \{z:\psi_\delta(P^{\mathrm F})\in\mathcal C_n(z)\} = \Gamma(P^{\mathrm F})^{\otimes n} \{z:\theta_\delta(\Gamma(P^{\mathrm F}))\in\mathcal C_n(z)\}.\] Therefore, using surjectivity again, \[\inf_{P^{\mathrm F}\in\mathcal M^{\mathrm F}} \Gamma(P^{\mathrm F})^{\otimes n} \{z:\psi_\delta(P^{\mathrm F})\in\mathcal C_n(z)\} = \inf_{P\in\mathcal M} P^{\otimes n}\{z:\theta_\delta(P)\in\mathcal C_n(z)\}.\] Thus the uniformly honest interval procedures are exactly the same for the causal and observed criteria. Their length objectives also agree: \[\sup_{P^{\mathrm F}\in\mathcal M^{\mathrm F}} \int \operatorname{len}\{\mathcal C_n(z)\}\,\mathrm d\Gamma(P^{\mathrm F})^{\otimes n}(z) = \sup_{P\in\mathcal M} \int \operatorname{len}\{\mathcal C_n(z)\}\,\mathrm dP^{\otimes n}(z).\] Taking the infimum over the common feasible class and using Definitions 22 and 18 gives \[L_{n,\mathrm F}^{\star}(\delta,\alpha)=L_n^\star(\delta,\alpha).\]

∎
Proof of Theorem 4.

  1. For standard Borel spaces \(S,T\) and every probability law \(\varrho\) on \(S\times T\), the usual disintegration gives a probability kernel \(K_\varrho:S\rightsquigarrow T\) such that, for measurable \(D\subseteq S\) and \(E\subseteq T\), \[\varrho(D\times E) = \int_D K_\varrho(s,E)\,d(\varrho\circ \operatorname{pr}_S^{-1})(s).\] This standard Borel disintegration supplies the regular conditional law needed for the observed-margin comparison in Proposition 3. Let \[\Gamma(P^{\mathrm F}) = \text{the observed }(X,A,Y)\text{-margin of }P^{\mathrm F}.\] Under the regime conditions, Proposition 3 gives surjectivity of \(\Gamma\) from \(\mathcal M^{\mathrm F}\) onto \(\mathcal M\) and, for every \(n\) and every \(\delta\in[0,\bar\delta]\), \[R_{n,\mathrm F}^\star=R_n^\star, \qquad L_{n,\mathrm F}^\star=L_n^\star .\] For every \(P^{\mathrm F}\in\mathcal M^{\mathrm F}\) and every such \(\delta\), Proposition 2 gives the target identity \[\psi_\delta(P^{\mathrm F}) = \theta_\delta\{\Gamma(P^{\mathrm F})\}.\]

  2. Let \(c_{\mathrm{risk}},C_{\mathrm{risk}}\) be the constants supplied by Theorem 1, and let \(c_{\mathrm{len}},C_{\mathrm{len}}\) be the constants supplied by Theorem 2. Set \[c=\min\{c_{\mathrm{risk}},c_{\mathrm{len}}\}, \qquad C=\max\{C_{\mathrm{risk}},C_{\mathrm{len}}\}.\] Then \(0<c<C\). Choose \(n_\star\) so that, for every \(n\ge n_\star\), the eventual conclusions of Theorem 1, Theorem 2, and Lemma 10 hold, and also \(n\ge4\). Fix such an \(n\), and write \[\delta=\delta_n, \qquad h=h_n, \qquad r=r_n=n^{-1/2}+\delta^{\kappa+1}h^\beta .\] Since \(0\le\delta\le\bar\delta\), Lemma 10 gives \[0<h, \qquad h\le 1-\bar\delta, \qquad n h^{2\beta+1}(\delta+h)^\kappa=1,\] and hence \[\delta+h\le \bar\delta+(1-\bar\delta)=1.\] Moreover \(r\ge0\).

  3. The total-Gram estimator in Algorithm 1 is a measurable function of the observed sample, so \[z\longmapsto \widehat\theta_n(z)\] is observed-sample measurable. The stabilized interval in Algorithm 2 has measurable endpoints built from the same finite sums and local-polynomial weights. The center is clipped to \([0,1]\), and the displayed radius is nonnegative, so the clipped lower endpoint is no larger than the clipped upper endpoint. Thus \(\mathrm{CI}_n\) is an observed-sample measurable interval.

  4.
